# Multi-fidelity physics worklog

The local Rust/Bevy robot can carry rigid parcels, weigh a loose cable and place a folded sheet once it measures sustained table support. The two finest load-confirmed placements stayed within 0.0076 mm of each other. The earlier timed opening still differs by 0.328 mm, and the first bounce after a receiver drops away varies with step size. [Rigid parcel](measurements/local-manipulation/serial-transfer.md) · [Cable](measurements/local-manipulation/serial-cable-weigh.md) · [Sheet placement](measurements/local-manipulation/sheet-supported-setdown.md) · [Receiver withdrawal](measurements/local-manipulation/sheet-receiver-drop.md)

Each new development phase includes visual output from the measured simulation and directions for viewing or reproducing it.


## 2026-11-23 — A push at a material point survives wrist promotion and replay

The CPU journal now records off-center forces on articulated links and model tier changes. A parked wrist struck by a 120 N force resumed with the same angular speed as the continuously dynamic control to within 2 × 10⁻¹⁵ rad/s per axis. On a rotating payload the moment correctly changed with the material point. Each of the four changed crates exceeded 95% for executable lines and regions in the scoped qualification. [Two robot fixtures, native playback and measurements](measurements/local-manipulation/point-load-replay.md)

## 2026-11-23 — Curved rigid bodies in the CPU journal and viewer

Two tilted parts with equal overall dimensions hit the ground eight milliseconds apart. The measured landing times matched their different flat and rounded ends within one tick. Their true mesh geometry now travels inside the native recording. The 600-step runs matched after reset and across thread counts. [Watch both contacts and inspect the measurements](measurements/local-manipulation/curved-body-replay.md)

## 2026-11-23 — A sliding material stops without reversing downhill

On a 30% incline the upstream strip stopped after the predicted 2.5 µm. The previous solver sent it 500 mm downhill within one second. The downstream control and four full folded-sheet simulations reproduced their prior trajectories. Older material checkpoints preserve their original equations. All 234 active solver tests passed and every changed executable line and region was covered. [Measurements, resolution limits and native before-and-after recordings](measurements/local-manipulation/uphill-slip-arrest.md)

## 2026-11-23 — The gripper retains the sheet when a loaded receiver drops during early opening

When the finite bench dropped 50 mm eight milliseconds into opening, the robot paused the jaws on the next actuator tick. They retained the 8.4 g folded sheet through the 4 mm search limit. After 40 ms of opening the fingers were already unloaded. The sheet then hit the lower bench after 101 ms, matching free fall at both solver rates. Its later bouncing and final opening time still varied with the solver interval. [Experiment, replays and remaining limitation](measurements/local-manipulation/sheet-receiver-drop.md)

## 2026-11-23 — The robot transfers sheet weight before opening

A 1 ms controller now lowers the gripper until its receiving surface carries the folded sheet for twenty consecutive observations. It took about 1.12 mm of additional travel to eliminate the free drop measured with timed opening. When the physical bench moved 262.5 mm aside, the jaws held the 8.4 g sheet and stopped their search after 4 mm. Both outcomes repeated after reset. [Measured loads, convergence, coverage and three native gz-web-rs recordings](measurements/local-manipulation/sheet-supported-setdown.md)

## 2026-11-23 — Replaying both axes of a screw-mounted wrist

A screw and a two-axis wrist now accept independent one-step loads in the local CPU journal. All three encoders responded on the first millisecond, pause retained the accumulated efforts and reset reproduced every one of the 180 repeated physics steps. All measured changed lines and regions were covered in the three crates. [Native recordings, control and measurements](measurements/local-manipulation/screw-universal-replay.md)

## 2026-11-23 — Gravity compensation holds an eccentric wrist payload

The local robot can calculate the weight and turning moment of its downstream rigid links in the joint's own axes. Applying the opposite torque held a 1 kg offset payload within 0.00216 mm over 1.2 seconds. The same free wrist moved 205.14 mm and swung 35.46°. Reset reproduced every calculated torque. Closed loops and conflicting parentage are refused. Contact and acceleration loads still require separate sensing or dynamics. [Example, native replay and four-crate coverage](measurements/local-manipulation/gravity-wrist.md)

## 2026-11-23 — GPU fingers grasp a finite rectangular payload

An 80 g cuboid lifted 96.19 mm clear of the table on the GPU and 96.79 mm in independent 3D Rapier. Tilting it initially, shifting its mass, missing the target, reducing pad friction and opening in midair produced distinct measured outcomes. A normal SDF run also handed the box to the receiving shelf. The freely rotating reference departed from planar motion during the tilted pinch and individual finger loads differed despite matching net weight. The flat single-world case reached 791 observed steps/s. [Contact geometry, measurements, coverage and gz-web-rs replays](measurements/local-manipulation/gpu-box-pickup.md)

## 2026-11-23 — Cloth on a translating turntable

A rectangular turntable carried its inner cloth through a three-radian turn while the outer cloth slid off at 160 ms. Both material resolutions agreed on the release tick, with a maximum 0.060 mm difference in the outer trajectory. Measured deck forces and moments matched the cloth momentum balance through release. This uses axial rotation and one-way contact. A freely tipping support with two-way cloth loads remains open. [Measurements, tests, coverage and gz-web-rs playback](measurements/local-manipulation/cloth-turntable.md)

## 2026-11-23 — Cloth on a moving finite deck

A deck moving at 0.4 m/s carried one 100 g sheet while a second slid off and fell. The sliding sheet lost its last measured support after 230 ms. While fully supported, it tracked the analytical solution within 0.037 mm. Reset reproduced both trajectories and the signed deck loads. A rotating or accelerating deck remains outside this straight-line qualification. [Measurements, coverage and native gz-web-rs replay](measurements/local-manipulation/cloth-conveyor.md)


## 2026-11-23 — GPU pickup with an off-center payload mass

The local four-slider gripper resolves the free sphere's authored mass center and central inertia independently of its geometric center. Shifting the mass center of an 80 g sphere 6 mm to the right shifted settled left/right support from 0.392/0.392 N to 0.285/0.499 N. Independent 3D Rapier measured 0.280/0.505 N. The successful pickup paths agreed within 1.08 mm. At friction 0.12 both spheres slipped back to the table, with a 17.80 mm right-pad slide on the device and 16.64 mm in Rapier. [Measured loads, limits, coverage and two native visual replays](measurements/local-manipulation/gpu-eccentric-payload.md)

## 2026-11-23 — GPU gripper carries a free object to a shelf

A fourth physical slider now carries the closed gripper 180 mm to a separate finite shelf. The 80 and 200 g spheres remained supported while moving and passed a 24 ms shelf-load test before opening. Their full paths stayed within 1.60 and 1.80 mm of independent Rapier. Frictionless pickup, a missing shelf and loading the wrong surface all inhibited release. A deliberately timed opening dropped the ball from 82 mm above the shelf. The qualified mechanism is planar. Off-axis contact and arbitrary articulated wrists still require development. [Measurements, controls and replay](measurements/local-manipulation/gpu-shelf-transfer.md)

## 2026-11-23 — Native joint readings on the local CPU transfer robot

The rail and both jaws publish real measurements on the standard joint-state topic at 100 Hz. A Zenoh subscriber verified the three prismatic joint types, timestamp and motion. The 200 g shelf transfer produced the same 451 visual frames as the run without the publisher. [Measurements, source coverage and gz-web-rs replay](measurements/local-manipulation/cpu-gripper-joint-state.md)

## 2026-11-23 — 2,048 observed GPU grippers

The ordinary SDF and Bevy path now runs 2,048 independent three-slider grippers with free objects and per-robot joint readings. It accepts a complete live action vector transactionally and publishes every robot each millisecond. Across eight load, friction and command scenarios, 1,024 spheres cleared 100 mm throughout the hold and the other 1,024 stayed on or returned to the table. Sixteen independent native CPU simulations checked both ends of the command range. Median throughput was 382,888 environment-steps/s with SDF commands and 301,212 with dense live commands, with identical physical and joint-sensor digests through all twelve episodes. [Timing, controls, coverage and visual replay](measurements/local-manipulation/gpu-gripper-population.md)

## 2026-11-23 — Limit repeated cloth collision searches

Bounded collision-search retries reduced CPU time for the damped and spring-only grippers in two paired campaigns. Damped p95 improved in both. Spring-only p95 was effectively unchanged in one and rose in the other. Discarding an expired reference immediately took more time in direct comparison, so the retained solver uses the bounded retry policy. All 64 follow-up runs reproduced the physical and viewer output byte for byte for each grasp. [Timings, coverage and replay](measurements/local-manipulation/cloth-clearance-cost.md)

## 2026-11-23 — Material sticks to a slope and a released sheet lands closer to its reference

A strip stayed stationary on a 25% slope, while a lower-friction strip accelerated to its predicted speed. Giving the folded sheet a separately authored static friction coefficient reduced its 500-vs-1,000-exchange trajectory difference from 210.89 to 81.43 µm. A further 2,000-exchange holdout differed from the 1,000-exchange run by 367.66 µm. Full release convergence remains open. The public-clock gripper matched 54,400 short native steps through the full grasp and release. The incline also exposed a 13.324 mm sliding-distance error at a 10 ms step, which motivates the next contact check. [Measurements, replays and source coverage](measurements/local-manipulation/material-incline.md)

## 2026-11-23 — GPU parallel jaws lift a free sphere

A local GPU gripper closed two moving fingertips around a free sphere, lifted it clear of the table and opened to release it. The GPU and independent Rapier simulation both held the 80, 200 and 350 g spheres for the full 141 ms evaluation window at a pad friction coefficient of 0.6. Their largest trajectory differences were 0.635, 0.706 and 1.760 mm. Open and frictionless jaws left the sphere on the table. The lower-friction transition remains sensitive to solver resolution, and extending these tests to a gripper on a moving arm is still open. [Comparison, native viewer and coverage](measurements/local-manipulation/gpu-parallel-gripper.md)

## 2026-11-23 — GPU rolling and table-supported retention

The local GPU arm now pushes a free rigid sphere, transfers frictional impulse and spin, and holds it against the table. A 0.024 N·s sideways disturbance shifted the held sphere 0.295 mm from its undisturbed twin, compared with 22.688 mm with the tool raised. Both independent Rapier and GPU runs loaded the sphere at 30 ms. Their maximum held-sphere path difference was 0.801 mm. The rolling controls matched the analytical speed, spin and energy loss. A separate oblique strike differed by 39.95 mm, and a free lift between moving jaws remains open. [Measurements, replay and coverage](measurements/local-manipulation/gpu-rolling-sphere.md) · [First free-object impact](measurements/local-manipulation/gpu-free-object.md)

## 2026-11-23 — Design review and implementation roadmap

The serial arm clears the barrier with loads up to 1.245 kg at the tested friction. It safely set down all eight diagonally weighted parcels that slipped, with no alarms in eight undisturbed controls. The folded-sheet arm now has measured timestep and individual jaw-force limits on light grippers. Recent qualification covers shelf placement at four physics rates, cable snag recovery, the loaded foot crossing a deck edge, and GPU-arm impact from a matched incoming state. The CPU fleet throughput and the full release coverage gate remain open. [Shelf placement](measurements/local-manipulation/serial-convergence.md) · [Loaded ankle](measurements/local-manipulation/compliant-ankle-edge.md) · [GPU impact](measurements/local-manipulation/native-landing-passes.md) · [Release coverage](measurements/local-manipulation/local-implementation-gate.md)

The [approved technical design](../../physics_tdd.md) defines five acceptance milestones. The table records the evidence for each, and the phases below give the proposed implementation order.

Committed ray hits, public IDs, force and impulse handling, simulation time, pause and reset have regression coverage. [Scene](../examples/multifidelity_platform.sdf) · [Headless and server checks](../artifacts/multifidelity/final-server.log) | The initial scene meets its acceptance criteria. Integrating the implementation from this physics worktree into the ongoing Gazebo port and qualifying the combined build are separate delivery steps. |
| **2. Switching and fleet** (§§4.1, 5.2, 8.2) | A moving, bent revolute assembly with offset inertia completed 10,000 round trips between tiers. Zone hysteresis, support loss, held inputs and contact history have dedicated checks. Authored cubic paths and selective native contact also work. With 100,000 decks, 1,000 loads, an exact range reading and full observations, the native index reduced step p95 from 40.32 to 16.08 ms. Throughput reached 0.89× real time. **Contact models available to robot scenes.** The welded foot and equivalent single rigid body agree at their first ground impact from a matched state. A loaded welded upper link also strikes a finite ledge, and a freely moving revolute ankle transmits a measured shank moment through a compliant sole. Rocking and changing-support fixtures cover impulse continuity. Current ankle work addresses crossing the finite support edge and simultaneous shank contact. [Revolute ankle](measurements/local-manipulation/compliant-ankle.md) · [Upper-link impact](measurements/local-manipulation/compliant-upper.md) · [Compliant soles](MULTIFIDELITY_LOCAL_SOLE_PHASES.md)

**Replay and release.** The local CPU gripper reproduced 596 trace records in separate one- and eight-thread processes through spawn, removal and reset. A changed impulse altered the first resumed solve. Changing the noise seed altered only sensor readings. The profile records the executable, build, CPU, numerical settings and input journal. The previous crate-wide Rapier run exceeded 95% on lines and regions. The full combined candidate still needs independent per-profile coverage and hosted qualification. [Local CPU replay](measurements/local-manipulation/local-cpu-input-replay.md) · [Rapier coverage](measurements/local-manipulation/rapier-crate-coverage.md) · [Combined release gate](measurements/local-manipulation/local-implementation-gate.md)

The next single-node work targets a second shelf contact and sustained support of a rotating GPU cuboid, safe handoff of a folded sheet to a supporting table, and the local CPU fleet throughput target. The local CPU wrist now parks under gravity, wakes on the first torque or impact, compensates for an eccentric load, and replays torque commands across pause and reset. The four-slider GPU gripper places 80 g and 200 g spheres and an 80 g finite cuboid on a shelf, and the separate 2,048-gripper population cleared its throughput target with full joint sensing. The 100,000-entity CPU throughput target and the combined release-coverage gate remain open. [Spherical wrist](measurements/local-manipulation/hanging-ball-wrist.md) · [GPU shelf transfer](measurements/local-manipulation/gpu-shelf-transfer.md) · [Rotating cloth deck](measurements/local-manipulation/cloth-turntable.md) · [Individual table impacts](measurements/local-manipulation/cloth-table-particles.md)

### Proposed phases and completion criteria

| Order | What to implement | How we will know it is ready |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Grasp limits and recovery** | Vary parcel mass, center-of-mass offset, surface friction and carry acceleration. Use jaw motion and contact reactions to detect slip and command a regrasp or controlled set-down. | Map successful and failed grasps. Compare the detection tick with measured parcel motion, record false alarms, drops, placement error and p95 step cost, and replay a successful carry, a recovery and a load beyond the gripper's capacity. |
| **2. An articulated arm using the gripper** | Mount the gripper on a multi-joint SDF arm. Complete the joint drive, speed-limit, damping and spring behavior needed by the model. Exercise collision between arm links, table and payload, with a clear-path control. | Reach, grasp, carry around an obstacle, place and reset with force-limited actuators. Compare joint limits, anchor drift, inertia response, collision clearance and pose error at 0.5, 1 and 2 ms. Measure free motion and contact separately across increasing link counts. Use that evidence to decide whether Rapier needs an additional algorithm. |
| **3. Local multi-fidelity robot and port integration** | Switch eligible assemblies during approach, support loss and settling. Preserve commands and two-sided contact state, and defer a switch that would lose a load. Coordinate reviewable local slices with the Gazebo port owner. | Compare switched and always-dynamic runs at matched states for first contact impulse, pose, twist, momentum, energy, command delivery and cost. Reproduce reset and replay through the normal SDF/CLI entry point. Run the integrated repository's normal, coverage and hosted checks. **This is the local rigid-robot delivery milestone.** |
| **4. Deformable manipulation** | Use the same arm to lift a cable and a folded sheet. Calibrate the material against sag, stretch and supported weight. Exercise one-way boundaries and two-way gripper reactions. | At 1, 2 and 4 soft substeps, report grip retention, penetration, strain, rigid and soft momentum, and energy across a declared stiffness and mass-ratio range. Show release and a failed-grasp control in gz-web-rs. |
| **5. CPU fleet target and replay profile** | Profile solver, broad-phase queries and Bevy publication separately. Exercise waypoint motion, swept trigger/overlap queries, changing contact density and the required sensor observations. Publish the deterministic CPU profile. | Rerun 100,000 entities on named hardware at fixed timestep and density. Report real-time factor, p95/p99 step time and memory with full observation. Check replay across worker counts and lifecycle edits. Record a pass or the remaining gap against 100×. |
| **6. GPU robot batches** | Port the measured robot workload that benefits from batching. Retain the CPU reference and qualify each device and observation cadence used. | For 2,048 mixed environments, report CPU/GPU pose and impulse errors, repeatability, end-to-end environment-steps/s, simulated seconds per wall second, p99, memory and transfer/fence costs. Check the 10,000 steps/s target on this workload. |
| **7. Optional distributed deployment** | When a single-node capacity need warrants it, run the existing protocol on two physical hosts, then extend spatial partitioning and swept halo bounds to the required assemblies and deformables. | Compare boundary contacts and migration during promotion with the single-node run. Inject late, duplicate and lost messages and physical-host outages. Verify unique ownership, restored impulses and recovery latency before expanding placement or node count. |

Each phase should leave a runnable Rust example or benchmark, recorded measurements and a gz-web-rs replay of the relevant behavior. New volumetric materials such as foam or soil can be scoped after cable and cloth acceptance if a robot task needs them. Photorealistic rendering, sensor raytracing and full fluid dynamics are outside the initial physics TDD.

## 2026-11-18 — First mixed CPU world

The physics development branch starts at simulator commit `8f13dc7`, after D33. The other Gazebo port work continues in its own checkout. The design document `physics_tdd.md` belongs to the parent Rust workspace. The approved design uses one opt-in Bevy world backend, Rapier f64 contact ownership, and explicit model-level fidelity. The public clock, IDs and ECS pose commit remain simulator responsibilities.

### Run the first example

Run this command from the physics worktree.

```sh
cargo run --locked -p gz-sim-cli --bin gz-sim-rs -- examples/multifidelity_platform.sdf --physics-engine multifidelity --server --run --iterations 500
```

The public timestep is 1 ms. A 1.2 kg box drops 4 cm onto a deck moving at 1 m/s. The deck supplies an infinite-mass boundary in the same Rapier f64 contact solver as the box. The CLI prints the final simulation time and link positions. At 500 steps the measured positions were 0.500 m along X for the deck and 0.378 m along X, 0.300 m above the world origin for the box. Omit `--server --run` to run the same scene without Zenoh.

The model-level `gz::sim::systems::PrescribedMotion` configuration in the example specifies both linear velocity (world axes, at the center of mass, m/s) and angular velocity (world axes, rad/s). Currently it applies to a top-level, nonstatic, single-link model with one centered primitive collider and no joints or nested models. Ordinary fixed and dynamic models in the world use the existing rigid-body construction path. Select the engine explicitly on the command line. Use the free-link world-velocity command to replace a prescribed twist. The scene command takes velocity at the link origin and converts it to center-of-mass velocity for the solver. Applied wrenches are refused on a prescribed link. Reset restores the initial twist. The first slice uses the existing public timestep and the pinned solver's integration settings.

The current CPU solver admits prescribed COM speed up to 400 m/s at its default length scale and angular travel up to π/4 radians per public step. SDF construction and queued velocity replacement validate these limits against the selected timestep. The angular integrator approximates constant rotation. At 1 rad/s for one simulated second the measured angle errors were 5.21 × 10⁻⁵ radians with a 100 ms step and 1.30 × 10⁻⁵ radians with a 50 ms step.

### View the moving platform in gzweb-rs

The example now gives both box visuals explicit opaque diffuse materials. The deck is blue and the cargo is orange. The original example stopped at viewer startup with `gzweb visual has no material`. After the change the simulator admitted the scene, opened its loopback viewer socket and reproduced the 500-step headless positions above.

For this checkout the checksum-verified M65 preview viewer files are extracted under `target/platform-viewer/gz-preview-m65-linux-x86_64/web`. Start these commands in separate terminals from the physics worktree.

```sh
target/debug/gz-sim-rs examples/multifidelity_platform.sdf --physics-engine multifidelity --server --web-viewer 9002
```

```sh
python3 -m http.server 8081 --bind 127.0.0.1 --directory target/platform-viewer/gz-preview-m65-linux-x86_64/web
```

Open the [local viewer](http://127.0.0.1:8081/?backend=primitive&ui=full&url=ws://127.0.0.1:9002/), click **Play** to start the paused simulation, and use **Reset** to replay the drop. The checkout's Rust binary supplies the simulated world. The HTTP process serves the prebuilt viewer files. Both listeners were verified locally. Automated visual inspection was blocked by the browser's localhost permission.

### Implementation and verification record

| Work item | State | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Design and plan | Revised | Parent-workspace TDD specifies time, COM conventions, assembly handoff, contact impulse accounting, replay and distributed ownership |
| Mixed CPU physics | Implemented | New backend installs through the existing Bevy resource and shares contact geometry and queries across tiers |
| Headless SDF integration | Exercised | Loaded the same example via the headless CLI and loopback Zenoh server, compared final output |
| Lifecycle and repeatability | Exercised on the example | Friction transfers horizontal motion to the falling box. Ray queries follow the deck. Paused time, reset IDs and committed replay bits are checked |
| Input and numerical edge cases | Exercised | Finite out-of-range speeds, COM conversion, queued input preservation, angular convergence, first IMU observation, and numerical step faults |

The solver adapter, SDF/CLI admission and Rust regression fixtures have separate file owners. The integration owner maintains the workspace manifest, lockfile, design, worklog and consolidated verification. The lockfile adds the local crate and its scene dependency.

The first server test reached the Zenoh listener and was denied a loopback socket by the build sandbox. With loopback enabled, the server test and three scene tests passed using the same source example and pinned dependencies. The Rust targets are named `multifidelity_platform` in both `gz-sim-cli` and `gz-sim-scene`.

The first wide regression run exposed a false assumption about extreme velocity. The pinned solver can cap the speed and return a finite stationary state. The correction preflights prescribed twists, retains pending commands on refusal, and reports an actual solver quarantine as a terminal step fault before publication. The direct Rust backend suites passed all 10 tests. The additional scene checks cover unsupported configurations, origin/COM conversion, initial and reset IMU readings, preserving pending inputs on refusal, and validating a prescribed turn at the selected public timestep. The checks use the pinned Rust 1.97.1 toolchain.

The focused LLVM coverage run measured 274/274 executable lines and 276/277 regions in the new crate. The report covers the new backend only. Local commands and output are retained under the worktree's ignored `artifacts/multifidelity/` directory. To repeat the coverage check:

```sh
cargo llvm-cov --locked --offline -p gz-physics-multifidelity --json --summary-only --output-path target/multifidelity-coverage.json
```

Its required external C++ Zenoh reference SDK is absent. The SDK bootstrap requires Ubuntu 24.04 amd64 with pinned packages. This host runs Ubuntu 26.04.1. The Rust loopback server test passed independently, including contact through 500 steps and exact output equality with the headless runner.
- Workspace formatting and `git diff --check` passed. Strict Clippy passed across all targets of the six selected crates. The `zenoh/unstable` feature supplies the listener inspection API used by existing CLI tests in the normal workspace test configuration.
- The first broad run had 1,002 passes and two failures. It identified the silent velocity clamp and the missing native SDK. The final run includes the repaired admission and new controls. The repository-wide normal, renderer, strict coverage and hosted qualification remain pending.
- Work is local to `physics/multifidelity`. The port branch has received no commit or merge from this worktree.

## 2026-11-18 — Assembly switching and fleet measurements

### Run an assembly switch

On this workstation the shell sets `RUSTC_WRAPPER=sccache`. If the wrapper reports `Operation not permitted` during Cargo's compiler version check, setting it to an empty value for this invocation runs `rustc` directly.

```sh
cd /home/nkoenig/work/gazebo/rust/gz-sim-rs-multifidelity
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo run --locked -p gz-sim-scene --example switch_articulation
```

The Rust program loads `examples/multifidelity_articulation.sdf`, accelerates the floating two-link robot, demotes it at 1 ms, carries it for 10 ms, promotes it at 11 ms, then applies joint effort. The root travels 0.001 m in the prescribed interval. At 21 ms the hinge angle is 0.00103343 rad and its angular velocity is 0.10736458 rad/s. The output includes the public model and hinge IDs, root pose, joint angle and angular velocity. Loading the SDF in the CLI starts the model in the dynamic tier. The example program makes the switch requests.

### Switching contract

- A top-level model opts in with the empty `gz::sim::systems::SwitchableArticulation` plugin named in the SDF example. The loader validates a floating revolute tree, constructs parent joints before children and preserves public joint declaration IDs. The physical root may differ from the canonical link.
- `set_model_fidelity` changes the whole loaded tree at a tick boundary. Both representations retain the same f64 reduced-coordinate joint state and public handles. Switch preflight checks ownership, internal motion, commands, velocity limits and external contacts before mutation. The adapter refreshes active contact manifolds after a successful switch. An active motor, moving hinge or pending dynamic wrench can defer demotion. A held wrench on a prescribed assembly acts once, over the first advancing dynamic step after promotion.
- In zero gravity, translation and principal-axis rotation can coast. Rotating transport uses the aggregate center of mass, including each link's own inertial offset. General torque-free tumbling currently defers. In gravity, the guard requires settled motion and measured contact on fixed support. Tests cover a falling robot, an arm supported by a dynamic pallet, and support removed before a switch.
- `gz_sim_scene::fidelity_zone::configure` installs a per-model policy with one or more world-space spheres. The committed root entering any inner radius triggers promotion. Demotion requires uninterrupted simulation time strictly outside every outer radius. Paused ticks consume no dwell time, a clock reset restarts dwell, and model removal retires its policy. The policy retries deferred transitions at later advancing boundaries. Explicit requests also work without a zone.

### Fleet workload

The release benchmark creates one Bevy entity per physics body and advances through the public physics schedule. Each 1 m square deck moves at 0.25 m/s. Sparse decks have 4 m spacing. The mixed grid has 1.1 m spacing and one friction-driven dynamic box per 100 decks. Each run warms up for 20 steps and measures 100 steps at a public 10 ms timestep. Final verification reads every pose, checks deck displacement and payload support/velocity, and counts active narrow-phase pairs. With `observe` a Bevy PhysicsCommit system also writes every body's pose and twist into its ECS component each tick. Timings exclude construction, the final checks, transport and rendering.

Before the translation optimization, the 100,000-deck sparse benchmark reached 0.278× real time with deferred readback. The mixed reference with 1,000 loaded decks reached 0.278×. In an exclusively translating world the backend now advances a shared integer clock. State and ray reads use the current translated pose. An edit or insertion materializes the elapsed interval before changing motion. A rotating, articulated, offset-COM or ordinary body selects the full solver. Tests verify that a payload added after 10 seconds of prescribed travel lands on the moved deck.

Measured on an AMD Ryzen 9 9950X3D, Linux x86_64, with CPU affinity 0–15. These are individual workstation runs. The pure deferred timing covers advancing the clock and Bevy schedule only.

| Decks | Active pairs | Full state written each tick | Real-time factor | p50 / p99 wall ms | Peak RSS KiB |
|---:|---:|:---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1,000 | 0 | No | 67,568× | 0.0001 / 0.0002 | 7,968 |
| 10,000 | 0 | No | 66,269× | 0.0001 / 0.0002 | 35,632 |
| 100,000 | 0 | No | 66,181× | 0.0001 / 0.0002 | 270,160 |
| 100,000 | 0 | Yes | 0.917× | 10.55 / 20.02 | 279,752 |
| 1,000 | 10 | No | 68.25× | 0.122 / 0.338 | 10,172 |
| 10,000 | 100 | No | 6.55× | 1.25 / 3.76 | 46,920 |
| 100,000 | 1,000 | No | 0.264× | 35.48 / 60.79 | 378,076 |
| 100,000 | 1,000 | Yes | 0.171× | 50.76 / 133.18 | 388,200 |

Maximum deck displacement error was 0 m against the closed-form fixture in the sparse runs and 6.54 × 10⁻¹² m in the largest mixed run. Reading and checking every deck at the end of the 100,000-deck sparse deferred run took 14.6 ms. The 100× target remains open for workloads with per-tick public state or interacting payloads. The next performance work is to profile the per-body lookup and ECS writes, then partition sparse contact work while preserving ray observations and collision response.

```sh
cargo build --release --locked --offline -p gz-physics-multifidelity --example fleet
/usr/bin/time -f 'peak_rss_kib=%M' taskset -c 0-15 target/release/examples/fleet 100000 4 0 observe
/usr/bin/time -f 'peak_rss_kib=%M' taskset -c 0-15 target/release/examples/fleet 100000 1.1 100 observe
```

Omit `observe` for deferred readback. Raw runs are in the local ignored directory `artifacts/multifidelity-phase2/`. The final ECS workload is recorded in `fleet-ecs.log`.

### Numerical acceptance

The independent loaded-scene suite runs through the public example SDF and public world-wrench queue. At the switch instant it checks both link frames and twists, hinge coordinates, simulation time and public IDs. Momentum, kinetic energy and the aggregate center of mass are independently reconstructed using each link's mass, inertia and rotated COM offset.

| Test | Measured result |
|---|---|
| 10,000 dynamic → prescribed → dynamic cycles, one 1 ms advancing step in each tier | Over 20.001 simulated seconds, maximum center error 1.05 × 10⁻¹¹ m, linear momentum drift 8.64 × 10⁻¹³ kg·m/s, angular momentum drift 8.49 × 10⁻¹² kg·m²/s, kinetic energy drift 1.12 × 10⁻¹³ J |
| Moving, motor-bent arm with residual principal-axis rotation, coasting for 50 ms | Center trajectory error 2.09 × 10⁻¹⁵ m, linear momentum drift 3.14 × 10⁻¹⁶ kg·m/s, angular momentum drift 2.85 × 10⁻¹⁵ kg·m²/s, kinetic energy drift 1.67 × 10⁻¹⁶ J |
| Moving-parent joint couple, same applied angular impulse over a fixed 4 ms observation window | Halving dt from 1 ms to 0.5 ms to 0.25 ms reduced linear momentum residuals from 4.73 × 10⁻⁶ to 1.99 × 10⁻⁶ to 9.04 × 10⁻⁷ kg·m/s. Angular residuals fell from 1.11 × 10⁻⁵ to 4.59 × 10⁻⁶ to 2.06 × 10⁻⁶ kg·m²/s. |

An early force-free rotating test exposed 1.06 μm of COM drift over 50 ms when only the root's motion was prescribed. Coasting about the aggregate COM corrected it. Removing a floor immediately before a switch also exposed stale narrow-phase handles. Demotion now rechecks measured support against live geometry and returns a typed deferral if the support moved or retired. The suite also exercises reset while a wrench is held and the first dynamic steps after promotion.

```sh
cargo test --locked --offline -p gz-sim-scene --test fidelity_switching -- --nocapture
cargo test --locked --offline -p gz-sim-scene --test fidelity_zones --test articulation_admission
```

## 2026-11-18 — Articulated and deformable CPU dynamics

### Run the physics examples

All three commands run from the isolated simulator worktree. `RUSTC_WRAPPER=` uses the local Rust compiler directly on hosts where sccache startup fails.

```sh
cd /home/nkoenig/work/gazebo/rust/gz-sim-rs-multifidelity
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo run --locked -p gz-sim-cli --bin gz-sim-rs -- examples/multifidelity_deformables.sdf --physics-engine multifidelity --iterations 300
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo run --release --locked -p gz-sim-scene --example humanoid_reach
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo run --release --locked -p gz-sim-scene --example garment_grasp
```

The first scene drops a 1 kg rigid sphere onto a cable pinned at both ends. A separate cloth panel hangs from two corners. At 300 ms the sphere center was 1.150872 m high, the lowest of the 11 cable particles was at 0.980872 m, and the lowest of the 25 cloth particles was at 0.607518 m. The CLI prints these measurements when the world has deformables. The public Rust API `gz_sim_scene::deformable::bodies(&sim)` exposes committed positions, velocities, per-boundary reaction impulses, time and epoch. The existing web viewer publishes rigid visuals. Soft mesh rendering and streaming remain open.

### Implementation

- The multi-fidelity SDF loader accepts finite bilateral stops on revolute joints, including world-anchored and floating trees. The interval must contain the initial coordinate of zero. The solver uses a continuous reduced joint coordinate, including beyond a full turn, and restores native stop constraints through reset and admissible tier switches. The actuator tests drive both directions under sustained effort and exercise bounded velocity and position motors.
- The Rust crate `gz-physics-xpbd` advances cables and triangulated rectangular cloth in f64. Cables use axial and two-edge distance constraints. Cloth uses warp/weft stretch, one shear diagonal per quad and signed dihedral bending about the rest configuration on interior edges. Compliance enters each substep through the squared substep duration. Earlier substeps receive any remainder nanoseconds. Pinned vertices retain their prescribed velocity. Self-contact projects non-neighbor particle pairs found in a spatial hash.
- The world plugin `gz::sim::systems::Deformables` registers optional Bevy systems. SDF particle positions are world metres in row-major order for cloth. A plane is a soft-only surface. A sphere references the fully scoped name of a real loaded link and its admitted collision radius. One-way boundaries sample the rigid center trajectory between its actual step endpoints and use sphere spin for tangential contact. A retired sphere drops out of the solve. Its reaction slot retains its declaration index. Loaded reset rebinds it.
- In two-way mode, soft bodies and finite sphere receivers share a substep clock. The solve accounts for native mass and angular inertia and shares a single receiver across contacting particles and soft bodies. It applies normal and friction corrections during the soft substeps and commits each corrected end pose and twist once. Static planes and prescribed boundaries continue to act as infinite-mass surfaces. Finite receivers currently require centered, unjointed dynamic spheres with isotropic inertia. The adapter checks corrected sweeps and measured third-body rigid impulses before updating Rapier. A simultaneous contact that would need a combined rigid/soft island faults the tick. The last published particle state and time remain available.
- The first implementation applied a reaction computed against an infinite-mass wall after the rigid step. A 0.1 kg cable striking a 0.01 kg sphere increased kinetic energy from 0.2 J to 2.0 J. The finite-mass solve removed this increase. A separate test confirmed that a speculative rigid contact in the gap between two fingers must allow cloth contact while a real solid obstruction must stop the correction.

### Numerical measurements

| Fixture | Result |
|---|---|
| Finite-amplitude pendulum released from 0.6 rad | Analytical period 2.056256902 s. Measured 2.056060997 s at dt=5 ms and 2.056217680 s at dt=1 ms. Maximum energy error fell from 0.839% to 0.169%, and pivot error from 2.67 µm to 0.107 µm. |
| Incline with default friction coefficient 0.5 | At 1 ms, the box drifted 13 µm with tan θ=0.4. With tan θ=0.8 its acceleration was 2.298068 m/s² against the analytical 2.298097 m/s². |
| Six 0.4 m cubes over the second simulated second | At 1 ms, the maximum vertical overlap bound was 2.288 mm, maximum height error 5.974 mm and maximum lateral drift 1.766 mm. |
| Joint stop at 0.5 rad under 1 N·m on 0.1 kg·m² | Peak penetration at 1, 2 and 4 ms was 1.21 × 10⁻¹², 7.00 × 10⁻⁵ and 8.60 × 10⁻⁴ rad. |
| Offset cable impact on a 4 kg sphere | For 1, 2 and 4 soft substeps per 1 ms rigid step, peak linear momentum residual was below 6 × 10⁻¹⁷ kg·m/s. Peak world angular momentum residuals were 8.08 × 10⁻⁸, 1.57 × 10⁻⁸ and 3.01 × 10⁻⁸ kg·m²/s. Kinetic energy stayed within the 0.2 J initial budget. |
| Cable-supported sphere | Over 0.65–2.65 s the 1 kg sphere oscillated between 1.086 and 1.189 m. The mean upward cable force was 9.12 N during this window. Integrated force minus weight matched the measured change in vertical momentum within 10⁻⁸ N·s. |
| Light-sphere radial impact | A 0.1 kg cable striking a 0.01 kg sphere retained a peak energy of 0.2 J from an initial 0.2 J. Direct solver fixtures also cover reversed mass ratios, multiple soft bodies sharing a receiver and offset friction impulses. |

Run the numerical controls from the same worktree.

```sh
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo test --locked -p gz-physics-xpbd
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo test --locked -p gz-sim-scene --test revolute_limits --test rigid_dynamics_qualification --test deformable_coupling -- --nocapture
```

### Manipulation demonstrations

- `humanoid_reach` generates and loads a 31-link branched SDF humanoid with 30 actuated revolute coordinates, a floating pelvis and zero gravity. Its torque-limited position servos drive a symmetric reach and hold for two simulated seconds at 1 ms. Both hands moved 0.4695 m forward, their separation decreased by 0.2213 m, and peak joint tracking error was 0.000221 rad. Peak aggregate COM drift was about 6 µm. The example accepts `-- --sdf` to print the neutral model.
- `garment_grasp` uses two independent finite-mass spherical fingertips in gravity. The control sequence closes, lifts, holds and releases a free 75 × 50 mm, 1.75 g cloth swatch. The open-finger control runs the same lifting motion. The swatch rose at least 87.70 mm during the hold and fell 112.43 mm after release. Both fingers carried contact for all 250 measured ticks. Measured vertical support was 0.017173 N versus the swatch weight of 0.017168 N. Minimum finger surface separation was 3.861 mm, peak pad/particle penetration was 0.036 mm and peak edge strain was 1.44%. The open-finger control measured zero fingertip support and contact.
- The two demonstrations exercise different mechanisms. The humanoid qualifies free-base articulated control. The gripper qualifies two-way fabric contact with measured squeeze force. Coupling contact feedback directly into articulated fingers remains open.

### Remaining engineering work

- Extend soft self-contact to edges and faces, qualify mesh-resolution-dependent bending compliance, and add cable twist/torsion. Current self-contact resolves particles within one soft body. Contact between independent cloth meshes, volumetric soft bodies, and garment mesh rendering/streaming remain open.
- Extend finite feedback to anisotropic bodies, articulated links and simultaneous third-body rigid contacts. The current coupled solve is limited to centered free spheres. Qualify freely moving supports, predicted-contact promotion, explicit SDF contact materials, and whole-garment grasp by an articulated hand.
- The next TDD milestone is Rust GPU batches. Compare CPU and GPU states and impulses, include packing/readback costs and qualify a specific device before claiming throughput. The CPU 100,000-body dense-observation and mixed-contact performance targets remain open. Distributed worlds and release qualification follow separately.

- The final 300-step deformable example produced byte-identical time, rigid pose and particle summaries through the headless CLI and the finite Zenoh server run. Both manipulation examples ran in release mode.
- The dedicated XPBD tests passed all 16 cases. LLVM coverage of the new solver crate was 880/907 executable lines (97.02%), 1,480/1,530 regions (96.73%) and 60/60 functions. Independent loaded-scene and backend fixtures covered 167/171 lines in the Bevy soft runtime, 269/283 in its SDF configuration module and 97/102 in the Rapier receiver adapter.
- A glancing-impact control exposed excess numerical spin on a sphere with tiny inertia. Correcting only the end spin caused a separate constrained cable impact to exceed its world angular momentum budget. The final solver passed both the exact frictionless glancing fixture and the unchanged loaded-scene momentum and energy budgets above.
- Workspace-wide compilation of all targets, workspace formatting, diff whitespace checks and strict all-target Clippy for the seven selected crates passed with Rust 1.97.1 on Linux x86_64. Normal and renderer release qualification and hosted promotion remain open. The work remains local to `physics/multifidelity`.

### Run and view

The vertical positions and sphere radii are in world metres. A [slowed animation captured from the running gzweb-rs viewer](../artifacts/multifidelity-phase4/phase4-batch.gif) is available locally. Browser checks exercised Play through the final frame and Reset back to zero, then disconnected the test browser.

From the isolated simulator checkout, this command runs the CPU comparison, repeatability checks and benchmark, and writes a new native gzweb archive. Select the target by its Vulkan adapter name. The tested build uses Rust 1.97.1 on x86_64 Linux.

```sh
cd /home/nkoenig/work/gazebo/rust/gz-sim-rs-multifidelity
mkdir -p target/gpu-batch-viewer
run_dir=$(mktemp -d target/gpu-batch-viewer/run.XXXXXX)
```

```sh
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo run --locked -p gz-sim-cli --bin gz-sim-rs -- "$run_dir/replay" --server --replay --web-viewer 9013
```

### Implementation and numerical scope

- The separate `gz-physics-gpu` crate owns a Vulkan compute pipeline and a Bevy resource. Its public `simulation::batch_simulation` constructor installs it in Gazebo's physics and reset phases with a 1 ms step. A successful full readback commits the new lanes and timestamp together. Paused ticks and failed steps preserve the last observation. Reset restores the input lanes, and the following step uploads them again.
- The Rust kernel builds typed Naga IR. Vulkan compiles it for the selected hardware device, which must support native f64 compute. Each invocation advances one independent vertical environment with a prescribed anchor, one bilateral XPBD distance constraint and one frictionless finite-mass sphere contact. The kernel uses the four-microstep free-flight endpoint from pinned Rapier, the constrained particle prediction, a swept normal projection and the equal-and-opposite reaction. Workgroups have 64 invocations and the last partial group checks its bounds.
- The resident storage ABI uses 12 contiguous f64 values (96 bytes) per environment. The host requests optional timestamp queries, checks device limits and reports the selected device. A GPU dependency tree check found no wgpu in the headless simulator CLI's normal dependencies.
- The independent CPU fixture steps the existing f64 Rapier backend and `gz-physics-xpbd::step_coupled` for every environment. The release qualification compared position, velocity and signed receiver impulse at every tick over 2,048,000 environment-steps. Both CPU and GPU reproduced their own full-state fingerprints at every tick after recreation or reset. The accuracy limits were 1e−8 m, 1e−6 m/s and 1e−6 N·s.
- Half the environments used stiff supports and half used more compliant supports. During the final 200 ms, the stiff group supplied 99.9962–100.0175% of the gravitational impulse on the rigid mass. The compliant group showed 3.484–149.524 mm peak-to-peak length excursions. Each compliant strut reversed axial velocity at least four times after impact.
- The initial fixture included a soft lane with too little excitation for the requested final-window oscillation witness. Its measured excursion was 0.94 mm. The revised soft cohort starts with a larger drop and also checks velocity reversals. The original numerical limits and excursion checks are retained. Early archive export also exposed a primitive-scene header mismatch. A round-trip test now exercises the recorder's native admission and checks each displayed rigid height.

### Verification

The maximum observed GPU differences from the general CPU solvers were 1.801e−13 m for position, 3.679e−11 m/s for velocity and 2.650e−11 N·s for receiver impulse. The largest rigid momentum residual over the final 200 ms was 2.04e−14 N·s. A separate 129-cell hardware control covers initial touch, swept contacts, 100 nm endpoint offsets, a partially filled workgroup, and 1:100 through 100:1 mass ratios.

Hardware tests exercised a destroyed GPU device, a failed readback, a nonfinite lane, reset and recovery, and the simulator fault path that blocks downstream publication. Focused LLVM coverage measured 621/634 executable lines (97.95%) and 892/919 regions (97.06%) across the new crate's production source. Workspace all-target compilation, workspace formatting, strict GPU all-target Clippy and whitespace checks passed. All 14 GPU crate controls, including the explicit hardware tests and replay admission test, passed. The Rapier, XPBD and multi-fidelity crates passed 147 release tests. Three independent release invocations produced byte-identical native replay streams and manifests.

### Throughput and transfer costs

The [three-way release benchmark](../artifacts/multifidelity-phase4/comparison.log) used a GeForce RTX 5090 (NVIDIA Vulkan driver 595.99.02) and a Ryzen 9 9950X3D. Each run advanced 2,048 separate environments at a 1 ms public step. The timed window was 900 ticks after a 100-tick warmup. Every row consumed all 12 f64 fields from every lane every tick. The specialized serial CPU implementation is a benchmark control using the same vertical equations as the GPU. Its maximum position difference against production Rapier/XPBD was 1.933e−13 m.

| Execution path | Environment-steps/s | Aggregate simulated seconds/wall second | Mean batch latency | p99 batch latency |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Production Rapier and XPBD on CPU | 1,733,801 | 1,733.8 | 1,181.2 µs | 1,526.6 µs |
| Matched vertical equations on one CPU thread | 49,835,663 | 49,835.7 | 41.1 µs | 45.6 µs |
| Vertical GPU kernel through Bevy, dense readback | 26,692,284 | 26,692.3 | 76.7 µs | 102.7 µs |

The GPU exceeded the 10,000 aggregate environment-steps/s target for this fixture. The matched CPU loop was 1.87× faster end to end. The GPU dispatch averaged 5.5 µs, device copy 5.9 µs, host wait and map 40.4 µs, decode and validation 7.4 µs, and full host observation 17.9 µs. The GPU timestamps overlap host submission and wait. A reseeded batch uploaded 196,608 bytes, with 0.10 µs host packing and 46.4 µs host upload. Resident ticks uploaded zero bytes and read back 196,640 bytes, including timestamps.

The explicit GPU buffers totaled 393,280 bytes. The device allocator reported 917,856 bytes allocated and 268,435,456 bytes reserved. Two preceding qualification runs with the same capture produced 18.22–26.99 million GPU environment-steps/s, with mean device-copy times of 5.9–29.8 µs. Reducing the frequency of host readbacks is a candidate for workloads that can consume several steps on the device. The measured profile publishes a dense snapshot on every tick.

### Remaining scope

The hardware result covers independent collinear contacts with one constraint, one finite sphere, one projection and a fixed tick. Arbitrary 3D articulations, cloth meshes, frictional rotation and contacts across GPU lanes still use other engine paths or require further GPU development. Further work includes GPU island scheduling for general geometry, additional devices, dense CPU fleet qualification, and the next TDD milestone of two-worker migration and boundary contacts. Full simulator release and hosted qualification remain open.

## 2026-11-18 — Phase 5, distributed ownership and boundary contact

[Open the handoff in gzweb-rs](http://127.0.0.1:8081/?backend=primitive&ui=full&lifecycle=1&replay=1&url=ws%3A%2F%2F127.0.0.1%3A9015%2F). Place the pointer over empty viewport space, press **F** to frame the scene and click **Play**. The white stripe identifies the incoming 1 kg box throughout. Cyan and orange identify the two owners. The division between the colored strips is the spatial partition at x = 0. The plinths and color panels are display geometry. The boxes fly in zero gravity.

### Reproduce

From the isolated physics worktree, build the worker executable and run the qualification. The example launches both workers, injects a lost acknowledgment and a process failure, runs two local controls, and verifies 200 physics steps before writing an archive. The recorder requires a fresh output directory.

```sh
cd /home/nkoenig/work/gazebo/rust/gz-sim-rs-multifidelity
mkdir -p target/distributed-viewer
run_dir=$(mktemp -d target/distributed-viewer/run.XXXXXX)
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo build --release --locked -p gz-physics-distributed --bin physics_worker
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo run --release --locked -p gz-physics-distributed --example distributed_handoff -- --capture "$run_dir/replay"
```

The running replay server uses `target/distributed-viewer/replay-panel` on port 9015. To view a new capture alongside it, use a free port, for example 9017, and [open that replay](http://127.0.0.1:8081/?backend=primitive&ui=full&lifecycle=1&replay=1&url=ws%3A%2F%2F127.0.0.1%3A9017%2F). The M65 viewer on HTTP port 8081 is shared with the earlier phases.

```sh
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo run --locked -p gz-sim-cli --bin gz-sim-rs -- "$run_dir/replay" --server --replay --web-viewer 9017
```

### Implementation and physical results

- The new optional Rust crate `gz-physics-distributed` runs two independent operating-system processes. Each has its own Bevy simulation, Rapier f64 backend and OS-assigned loopback TCP endpoint. Upstream Zenoh carries requests and replies outside the physics step. Local Bevy entity and native solver handles stay within the worker. Bodies retain a stable global ID across ownership changes.
- The coordinator reserves journal space for an entire operation, checks per-worker run/sequence/tick and publishes a frame only after both workers finish a barrier. Swept bounds that account for relative translation and the full possible rotational extent force co-location before potential cross-owner contact. One solver integrates both responding masses. The example initiates migration when the leading face reaches the partition.
- The source freezes the body, the destination prepares its native representation, the source retires the old owner and the destination activates the new generation. The protocol can retry an interrupted operation or fence both processes and return to the last published frame. A successful worker replacement must reproduce every acknowledged reply from its original seed before the coordinator resumes.
- At 0.85 s the incoming box's center was at −0.30 m. It changed from prescribed motion on worker 0 to dynamic motion on worker 1 at identical pose and twist. The example intentionally lost the release acknowledgment after native removal. The public observation held at the old ownership until retry succeeded. At 1.19 s the two boxes collided. Their final x velocities were 0.533332872 and 0.533333564 m/s from initial values of +2.0 and −0.2 m/s.
- The contact supplied 1.466667127748 N·s of normal impulse. The 2 kg receiver independently gained 1.466667127748 kg·m/s of x momentum. The net momentum residual was 2.22 × 10⁻¹⁶ kg·m/s. At 1.45 s the example killed the contact owner after its peer had stepped. A new process replayed from the seed through the impact, reconstructed the solver's contact history and completed the interrupted tick.
- Both local references used the same physics schedule. One promoted at the transfer barrier and one used dynamic physics for both bodies from the beginning. Each comparison checked all 200 ticks, including contact count and body IDs on each impulse. The maximum position difference was 2.14 × 10⁻¹⁴ m, linear velocity difference 1.37 × 10⁻¹⁴ m/s, angular velocity difference 4.58 × 10⁻¹⁴ rad/s, sign-invariant quaternion distance 8.26 × 10⁻¹⁶, and per-tick normal impulse difference 2.71 × 10⁻¹⁴ N·s.
- The native archive carries 54 observed frames at their simulation times, including both ownership observations at 0.85 s. The recorder rejected the first attempt to change color on an existing visual ID. The accepted archive uses constant materials and moves the inactive color panels inside each opaque box. The display rotates the world 90° around the vertical axis so the crossing is visible side-on. Two independent release runs produced byte-identical frame streams and manifests, including the recovery.

### Verification

The focused release suites passed 178 tests across the distributed, Rapier and multi-fidelity crates. The 45 distributed controls exercise each interrupted handoff stage, reordered and delayed requests, malformed and oversized wire messages, process failure, partial barriers, recovery divergence and rollback after retirement. The test-only child entry runs in its own Rust subprocess. The [focused coverage report](../artifacts/multifidelity-phase5/coverage.json) covered 1,102 of 1,126 production lines (97.87%) and 1,697 of 1,770 regions (95.88%) in the distributed crate. Strict Clippy passed for all targets in the three crates. Workspace all-target compilation, formatting and whitespace checks passed. These are local physics results. Hosted and full simulator release qualification remain open.

A second fixture migrated a dynamic box before a glancing collision. Across three simulated seconds, the migrated and uninterrupted native runs produced identical sampled pose, twist and contact impulses. Both generated 1.13918 rad/s of spin and both drifted from 0.608000 to 0.602125 kg·m²/s in total angular momentum at the 10 ms public step. An initial absolute error check of 0.001 kg·m²/s failed on the uninterrupted solver itself. Refinements to 5, 2 and 1 ms produced absolute errors of 0.000491, 0.003725 and 0.009158 kg·m²/s. The glancing case therefore qualifies migration parity. Angular-momentum accuracy under off-center contact remains an open numerical issue.

Chrome played all the way to 2.000 s and reset to paused frame 1 of 54. Viewport samples on either side of the handoff confirmed that the cyan surface became orange and the stripe stayed visible. The [slowed gzweb capture](../artifacts/multifidelity-phase5/handoff.gif) and the [two-worker qualification output](../artifacts/multifidelity-phase5/qualification.log) are available in the physics worktree.

### Remaining scope

This phase qualifies a fixed partition with two contact-free boxes at transfer, a trusted coordinator and two local child processes. Once an island has a contact history, this profile keeps it on its owner. Owner recovery rebuilds warm-start data by replaying the bounded in-memory journal. Cold snapshots cannot restore a previously contacting island. Durable checkpoints, migration of a complete warm contacting assembly with pending inputs, multiple machines and adaptive load balancing remain open. The coordinator currently exchanges step requests sequentially within each barrier. Overlapping independent work, persistent coordinator recovery and throughput at scale need separate qualification.

## 2026-11-18 — Warm migration of a contacting pair

[Open the two-worker replay in gzweb-rs](http://127.0.0.1:8081/?backend=primitive&ui=full&lifecycle=1&replay=1&url=ws%3A%2F%2F127.0.0.1%3A9017%2F). Hover over empty viewport space, press **F** to frame the pair, then **Play**. Both boxes start cyan on worker 0. At impact they change to orange together as worker 1 takes ownership. The white stripe identifies the lighter box that receives the external impulse. The gray platform is a display guide in this zero-gravity fixture.

### Run the qualification

The example starts two worker processes and two uninterrupted reference simulations. By default it drops the acknowledgment after the destination activates the island. Use `--reliable-transport` to deliver the acknowledgment on the first attempt.

```sh
cd /home/nkoenig/work/gazebo/rust/gz-sim-rs-multifidelity
mkdir -p target/warm-viewer
run_dir=$(mktemp -d target/warm-viewer/run.XXXXXX)
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo build --release --locked -p gz-physics-distributed --bin physics_worker
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo run --release --locked -p gz-physics-distributed --example warm_handoff -- --capture "$run_dir/replay"
```

The linked replay uses `target/warm-viewer/replay` on port 9017. It runs as the transient user service `gz-physics-warm-replay`. The earlier boundary replay is available on port 9015 under `gz-physics-boundary-replay`. To serve a newly recorded archive alongside it, start a replay server on port 9019 and [open that capture](http://127.0.0.1:8081/?backend=primitive&ui=full&lifecycle=1&replay=1&url=ws%3A%2F%2F127.0.0.1%3A9019%2F). The HTTP viewer on port 8081 is shared with the earlier examples.

```sh
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo run --locked -p gz-sim-cli --bin gz-sim-rs -- "$run_dir/replay" --server --replay --web-viewer 9019
```

### Implementation

- A fixed-step Bevy worker can queue ordered world-frame linear impulses at either box's center of mass. They take effect immediately before the next advancing solve. Admission checks body identity, dynamic ownership, a limit of 32 queued inputs and the pinned solver’s 400 m/s center-of-mass speed limit after each input. The coordinator records the accepted input order and checks the exported checkpoint against it before retiring the source.
- A whole-island checkpoint contains the two boxes' global-to-native ID mapping, exact public state, pending inputs and the serialized native Rapier world, including narrow-phase manifolds, warm-start accumulators, broad phase, active islands and solver parameters. Restoration recreates local Bevy bindings, validates the actual restored body/contact state against the public snapshot, and preserves the global barrier tick. This same-build codec is an optional native backend feature. The headless CPU server can continue to build independently.
- Export freezes the entire pair. Prepare constructs and validates a separate destination solver while the source retains ownership. Release retires both source bodies, then activation publishes both new generations. The two bodies can arrive with different prior generations. Phase-specific receipts bind the checkpoint and transfer ticket with SHA-256. Native data travels on export and prepare. The remaining acknowledgments use compact receipts. Transport restricts requests to a per-process capability delivered through the coordinator's child-process pipe and renewed on restart.
- The earlier cold, single-box path remains available before contact. Pending inputs and contact history make a partial transfer ineligible. An interrupted warm operation can resume from its acknowledged commands or roll back both workers to the preceding public frame. Worker process recovery continues to replay acknowledged history, including warm transfers.

### Qualification and numerical results

- At tick 119 (1.19 s), native contact had supplied 1.463645654474 N·s. The coordinator queued +1.2 N·s on the 1 kg trailing box and moved both active bodies to the empty worker before the next step. The native solver image occupied 6,955 bytes. One manifold contained eight contact points, four with positive retained normal impulses. The largest retained impulse was 0.00401485147 N·s. The recipient restored the contact state before applying the queued input.
- Over all 200 ticks, the migrating run matched the uninterrupted solver bit-for-bit in both bodies' poses and linear/angular velocities, contact counts and per-pair impulses. Relative to the unforced control, the 2 kg receiver gained 0.802630208089 kg·m/s. The contact supplied 0.805651681363 N·s after the handoff. The largest total momentum residual relative to one applied input was 6.66 × 10⁻¹⁶ kg·m/s.
- A separate glancing collision resumed on 36 active-contact barriers over 150 ticks. The largest observed native retained normal impulse was 0.043845195265 N·s and the largest absolute z angular speed was 1.139179436 rad/s. Restored warm-start impulse bits and all sampled body and contact observations matched uninterrupted execution. A sustained compressive fixture also resumed on 25 consecutive contact barriers. This qualifies preservation of the pinned solver's behavior. The absolute angular-momentum drift measured in Phase 5 remains open.
- A native one-shot wrench control restored a 2 kg box with 4 N of force and 1 N·m of torque pending. Its next linear and angular speeds reached 0.020 m/s and 0.120 rad/s, equal to the uninterrupted run. Both stayed constant on subsequent free steps.
- Failure tests interrupt each of export, prepare, release, activate and the final source observation. They also exercise a failed input acknowledgment, rollback, reverse migration, corruption of pending inputs and receipts, and replacement of both OS processes after a prior single-box promotion and a warm handoff.

The three affected crates passed 195 release tests. An additional 40 scene controls exercised the existing articulation, deformable, promotion and contact paths under instrumentation. Workspace all-target compilation, strict all-target Clippy for the affected crates, formatting and whitespace checks passed. The [combined local coverage report](../artifacts/multifidelity-warm/coverage.json) measures production source across these runs.

| Crate | Line coverage | Region coverage |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| `gz-physics-distributed` | 98.03% | 96.01% |
| `gz-physics-rapier` | 98.38% | 97.86% |
| `gz-physics-multifidelity` | 100.00% | 99.73% |

The [gzweb capture](../artifacts/multifidelity-warm/handoff.gif) shows both boxes changing owner in the same frame. Browser checks exercised Play through 2.000 s and Reset to paused frame 1 of 53. The [release qualification output](../artifacts/multifidelity-warm/qualification.log) includes the native contact impulses and reference measurements. Runs with and without the lost activation acknowledgment produced byte-identical replay streams and manifests.

### Remaining work

This implementation migrates the complete two-dynamic-box solver to an empty worker on the same host and build. General island extraction into an occupied destination, joints and deformables in a migrating assembly, cross-host qualification, persistent coordinator checkpoints and adaptive scheduling remain open. The journal still bounds run length and recovery cost. A useful next distributed milestone is moving one contact-connected group while unrelated bodies continue on each worker.

## 2026-11-18 — Selective handoff between populated workers

Two dynamic boxes now migrate together while each worker continues simulating an unrelated box. The coordinator takes either contacting body's global ID, finds its native solver and transfers both IDs at the same committed tick. Only the migrating bodies advance their ownership generations. All four keep their poses and twists through the transfer.

[Open the four-box replay in gzweb](http://127.0.0.1:8081/?backend=primitive&ui=full&lifecycle=1&replay=1&url=ws%3A%2F%2F127.0.0.1%3A9019%2F). Play through 1.19 s or scrub across the two samples at that time. The two center boxes change owner together. The upper and lower boxes continue on their original workers. The display compresses the 12 m vertical spacings to 1.15 m so all three tracks fit on screen. Horizontal displacements and box dimensions are in metres.

To build the Rust worker and record a fresh replay from the isolated physics checkout, run

```sh
cd /home/nkoenig/work/gazebo/rust/gz-sim-rs-multifidelity
mkdir -p target/selective-viewer
run_dir=$(mktemp -d target/selective-viewer/run.XXXXXX)
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo build --release --locked -p gz-physics-distributed --bin physics_worker
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo run --release --locked -p gz-physics-distributed --example selective_handoff -- --capture "$run_dir/replay"
```

The example drops the first activation reply after the destination has accepted both bodies. To deliver every reply, add `--reliable-transport`. The shared replay runs under the user service `gz-physics-selective-replay` on port 9019. To [view your fresh recording](http://127.0.0.1:8081/?backend=primitive&ui=full&lifecycle=1&replay=1&url=ws%3A%2F%2F127.0.0.1%3A9021%2F) through the same gzweb frontend, run

```sh
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo run --locked -p gz-sim-cli --bin gz-sim-rs -- "$run_dir/replay" --server --replay --web-viewer 9021
```

### Implementation

- A worker forms independent native Bevy/Rapier solvers from its seed and co-locates cold bodies before a potential local contact. Each solver tracks its own contact history. The worker publishes sorted member IDs, body states and per-pair contact observations at the same barrier tick.
- The coordinator checks all potentially interacting boxes across owners before it sends a step. Its shared admission code includes queued impulses, the box's orientation-independent bounding radius, and potential secondary collisions after a local impact. Dynamic bodies with solver contact history can change direction within the pinned solver's substep speed bound. An overlap that needs a warm solver merge stops the tick before integration.
- A selective handoff exports the native two-box checkpoint and only those bodies' ordered pending inputs. Prepare restores it separately from the destination's resident solvers. Release removes the selected solver at the source. Activation adds it at the destination. The coordinator checks both workers' resulting state, including the bystanders and all contact records, against the expected transfer before publishing. Unrelated native solvers keep their caches and pending commands.
- Each worker admits up to 32 distinct IDs over its lifetime, including retired IDs, and up to 32 queued impulses per tick. The coordinator checks both limits before export. Tests exercise lost replies after each transfer phase, fenced replacement, reverse transfer, rollback after corrupted bystander state, and admission refusal without consuming accepted input.

### Numerical results

The two worker processes ran 200 steps of 10 ms against one uninterrupted four-box native solver. All four body states and every contact impulse matched bit-for-bit at every step. At tick 119 the pair transferred with an active normal impulse of 1.463645654474 N·s. Its serialized native solver state occupied 6,955 bytes. The pair took a further 0.805651681363 N·s of contact impulse after the handoff.

| Body or group | Queued horizontal impulse | Measured result |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Contacting pair, IDs 1 and 2 | +1.200 N·s on ID 1 | ID 2 gained 0.802630208089 kg·m/s against the unforced control. Both IDs changed from worker 0 to worker 1. |
| Source bystander, ID 3 | +1.050 N·s | Horizontal speed rose from 0.65 to 1.35 m/s. ID 3 stayed on worker 0 at generation 0. |
| Destination bystander, ID 4 | −1.125 N·s | Horizontal speed changed from −0.85 to −1.30 m/s. ID 4 stayed on worker 1 at generation 0. |

The largest horizontal momentum residual against a separate no-input solve was 6.66 × 10⁻¹⁶ kg·m/s for the pair, 2.22 × 10⁻¹⁶ kg·m/s for ID 3 and zero for ID 4.

A six-box control kept three glancing contact pairs active. One pair migrated at tick 5 with a maximum retained warm-start impulse of 0.043845195265 N·s. The source and destination each retained a second, unrelated warm solver. Those two checkpoints, including their queued inputs, were byte-identical across the transfer. The combined six-body state and per-pair impulses matched a monolithic solve bit-for-bit over the next 120 ticks.

A separate 220-tick control used rotating cuboids with different initial orientations and angular velocities. Their swept bounds brought them into one solver at tick 71. They collided at tick 82 with a peak normal impulse of 0.542040694 N·s. Maximum absolute differences against the monolithic solver were zero for position, quaternion components, linear and angular velocity, and normal impulse on this build.

The [browser capture](../artifacts/multifidelity-selective/handoff.gif) shows the center pair changing from cyan to orange while both bystanders keep their colors. Browser checks exercised Play through 2.000 s and Reset to paused frame 1 of 53. The two real-process runs, with and without the lost activation acknowledgment, produced byte-identical replay archives. A fresh [release qualification run](../artifacts/multifidelity-selective/qualification.log) reproduced the served archive.

### Verification

On an AMD Ryzen 9 9950X3D running x86-64 Linux, Rust 1.97.1 passed 213 release tests across the distributed, multi-fidelity and Rapier crates. The test harness invokes a separate Zenoh helper as a subprocess. The workspace all-target check, strict all-target Clippy for those three crates, formatting and whitespace checks passed. The qualification used two local OS worker processes with Zenoh TCP loopback. It measures physical continuity and recovery on this build.

The [local LLVM coverage report](../artifacts/multifidelity-selective/coverage.json) combines the three physics crates' tests with 40 scene integration tests. Production-source coverage was

| Crate | Lines | Regions |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| `gz-physics-distributed` | 97.76% | 96.07% |
| `gz-physics-multifidelity` | 100.00% | 99.73% |
| `gz-physics-rapier` | 98.38% | 97.86% |

### Remaining work

The qualified unit of selective migration is one complete native solver containing two contacting, always-awake dynamic boxes. The solver must be independent of the other resident solvers. Larger contact groups, splitting a previously coupled native solver, and merging solvers that already carry contact history remain open. A conservative overlap can pause a step while those operations are unavailable. The next distributed milestone is to move a three-or-more-body contact group with its native caches and ordered inputs. Later work includes joints, deformables, multi-host transport qualification, durable coordinator recovery and load balancing.

## Overnight development, November 18–19, 2026 (Pacific)

The requested six-hour implementation window starts at 9:43 p.m. PST and ends at 3:43 a.m. PST. The separate morning report is due at 6 a.m. PST. Development is isolated in the `physics/multifidelity` worktree. The baseline is the selective two-box handoff qualified above.

### In progress at 9:44 p.m. — connected groups of dynamic boxes

Extend the native same-build checkpoint, Bevy partition, worker transaction and coordinator to handle a whole connected contact group of 2–32 dynamic boxes. Preserve ordered inputs and native contact data at a barrier. Qualify a three-box contact chain, unrelated residents on both workers, ordering and capacity refusals, interrupted transactions, and the maximum supported fixture. Deliver a real-process recording and gzweb replay. The next recovery milestone is to let a fresh coordinator resume a published barrier using persisted evidence.

### 9:52 p.m. — simultaneous-contact discrepancy reproduced

A symmetric 1.5 m-spaced three-box chain exposed an error before migration. A five-body monolithic solve and an isolated three-body solve agreed. At tick 100 the partition that co-located the three boxes during their approach produced a second active contact worth about 0.75662 N·s. The middle box reached 0.41502 m/s versus the reference's 0.79333 m/s. Both conserved the group's linear momentum. The pinned native solver had recycled a previously empty, inactive manifold on the monolithic path. The recently formed partition evaluated the contact afresh. Increasing the number of solver iterations did not remove the discrepancy. Work is underway to qualify fresh manifold evaluation for this distributed box profile while retaining normal-impulse warm starting. The original long-gap fixture remains a regression and visual qualification case.

### In progress — durable recovery of a published barrier

The coordinator can opt into recording accepted user actions and explicitly save an immutable recovery point. The file contains the two seeds, action history, and SHA-256 evidence over the worker requests, replies, published state, ownership serial and ordered inputs. A fresh coordinator regenerates native checkpoints by rerunning the worker protocol. File publication uses a synced temporary file, create-without-replacement, and directory sync. A save made after an ambiguous reply includes the preceding committed barrier. Local native-worker tests have recovered pending impulses and ownership generations across cold promotion, active contact, a lost activation reply and a subsequent worker restart. Separate OS process-loss tests, visualization and final qualification are in progress.

### Connected-group qualification

At 1.000 s three boxes migrated together between two populated OS workers. Both contacts were active, at 0.586666666666 and 0.756622222218 N·s. The native image was 10,409 bytes and contained two manifolds with 16 cached points. All five bodies and both contact impulses matched the uninterrupted native five-body control and a separate uninterrupted partitioned run bit for bit for 200 ticks. Dropping the activation reply produced a byte-identical replay. The [release log](../artifacts/multifidelity-contact-group/qualification.log), [gzweb preview](../artifacts/multifidelity-contact-group/handoff.gif) and [interactive replay](http://127.0.0.1:8081/?backend=primitive&ui=full&lifecycle=1&replay=1&url=ws%3A%2F%2F127.0.0.1%3A9023%2F) are available locally. Gzweb Play reached frame 52 at 2.000 s and Reset returned to frame 1.

A 32-box chain with 31 active contacts also transferred across two real loopback Zenoh workers. The export response occupied 318,896 bytes and the native solver image 119,015 bytes. Source and destination body and contact observations matched exactly. The protocol enforces separate limits for checkpoint-bearing packets, small control messages and seed data.

Fresh manifold evaluation fixed the long-gap discrepancy. Rebuilding a cold singleton now also uses the same native prescribed-body integration as a multi-body distributed solver. These two decisions apply specifically to the distributed awake-box construction profile. A like-for-like release benchmark measured the cost of fresh evaluation using identical initial native states, warm-up, and 2, 11 or 31 active contact pairs. Median native tick costs increased from 6.61 to 7.30 µs for three bodies, 15.18 to 22.06 µs for 12 and 29.53 to 48.84 µs for 32. Ordinary Rapier scenes retain their existing integration settings. Earlier entries describe the earlier build. For example, the previously qualified promoted pair now delivers 1.466666666667 N·s at tick 119.

```sh
cd /home/nkoenig/work/gazebo/rust/gz-sim-rs-multifidelity
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo build --locked --release -p gz-physics-distributed --bin physics_worker
RUSTC_WRAPPER= cargo run --locked --release -p gz-physics-distributed --example contact_group_handoff
```

### In progress — prospective contact routing

The coordinator now plans cold-body ownership changes before a predicted cross-worker collision. The planner considers all bodies' swept bounds together so secondary contacts can join the same prospective group. It retains any warm component and queued inputs on their current owner, minimizes the number of transfers, and checks the two workers' remaining distinct-ID budgets jointly across independent groups.
