### November 20 — Local CPU promotion before impact with a free payload

Contact promotion now accepts a free dynamic payload selected by public link ID. On each advancing tick, the Bevy system evaluates the current collision shapes and staged payload motion before the local Rapier solve. The predictor includes forces, gravity and angular motion. When another contact could deflect the payload into the arm during the step, it accounts for that possibility. The prescribed arm must have settled internal joints. [Predictor](../crates/gz-physics-rapier/src/contact_prediction.rs) · [Free-body motion bounds](../crates/gz-physics-rapier/src/contact_prediction/dynamic.rs) · [SDF example](../examples/multifidelity_free_payload_impact.sdf)

A 0.2 kg sphere struck the tip of a coasting two-link arm off center. The arm switched to dynamic at 203 ms. Both the automatic and always-dynamic worlds recorded the first positive normal impulse at 210 ms (0.489715 N·s), the same payload reversal from +2.000 to −0.407 m/s along X, and the same final hinge angle of −0.265553 rad. A second trajectory cleared the arm by 0.210 m and stayed prescribed for all 600 steps. All 601 momentum observations matched the reference. At 1 ms, both worlds had maximum absolute linear and angular momentum drift of 1.1924 × 10⁻⁴ kg·m/s and 1.5095 × 10⁻⁴ kg·m²/s. At 0.25 ms these decreased to 1.3800 × 10⁻⁵ and 5.0418 × 10⁻⁵ in the same units. [Measurements and reproduction](../artifacts/multifidelity-free-payload/README.md) · [Response plot](../artifacts/multifidelity-free-payload/response.svg)

A separate third-body test fired a striker into a stationary payload. The predictor requested promotion before advancing. The native solver delivered a striker–payload impulse on tick 1 and a payload–arm impulse on tick 2. The same striker 80 m away left the arm prescribed. The regular simulator CLI reached 600 ms with all three final positions equal to the recorded run. The Rust replay readback checked 440 visual poses and shapes, 44 frame times and both event markers. Chromium displayed the promotion and impact seven frames apart at 1 ms intervals around the collision. [gz-web-rs captures and playback](../artifacts/multifidelity-free-payload/view/README.md) · [Scene regression results](../artifacts/multifidelity-free-payload/checks/promotion.log)

**Remaining local work.** Tighten the bound for payloads already resting or sliding on a support. In the current floor test, the arm promotes at tick 75 for a hit at tick 234. It also promotes at tick 75 for a closer miss. A wider miss keeps the arm prescribed. The next qualification should distinguish sustained floor support from a new deflecting contact and retain pre-impact promotion for genuine secondary strikes. [Floor regression](../crates/gz-sim-scene/tests/contact_promotion.rs)

### November 21 — Twenty local CPU phases for a sliding payload and a supported arm

The new predictor distinguishes a single loaded planar support from a possible secondary impact. It checks current face geometry, both colliders' native contact skins, collision filters, the reachable region around the payload and the upcoming native solve mode. Edges, externally applied loads, additional reachable obstacles and unsupported CCD modes retain conservative promotion. The Bevy policy publishes the selected public link and the clock interval of a committed automatic promotion. A swept-box rejection avoids expensive shape queries for disjoint paths. [Twenty-phase ledger, results and reproduction](../artifacts/multifidelity-supported-payload/README.md)

The canonical strike switched at 227 ms instead of 75 ms. Both automatic and always-dynamic runs first delivered impulse at 234 ms (1.814247 versus 1.813226 N·s). Maximum center discrepancy was 1.010 mm. The near miss with 0.230 m of lateral clearance stayed prescribed. All nine strikes across three timesteps matched the reference contact tick. False promotions fell from nine to three of twelve passing runs. About 10 mm of lateral clearance put those three passes inside the native margin. A separate 0.25 rad tilted-world trial preserved the switch and contact ticks. At a 2 ms timestep, the normal guard admitted demotion after an additional 792 ms of settling. [Per-trial measurements](../artifacts/multifidelity-supported-payload/matrix.json) · [Event and response data](../artifacts/multifidelity-supported-payload/summary.json)

For 64 distant targets the p95 prediction query batch fell from 75.36 to 16.72 µs and 360 skew native casts agreed with the unpruned result. Median trial p95 for whole Bevy steps changed from 26.36 to 26.20 µs on the hit, from 22.96 to 21.37 µs on the near miss and from 25.55 to 24.40 µs with 32 additional fixed boxes. The matched timing ranges overlap. Two gz-web-rs replays show the previous and current behavior. Each includes a measured always-dynamic control and a separate miss. Chromium displayed all 49 frames in each and verified reset. [Timing trials](../artifacts/multifidelity-supported-payload/checks/timing) · [Replay links, plots and captures](../artifacts/multifidelity-supported-payload/view/README.md)

The selected release suites passed 557 tests. The ordinary simulator CLI reproduced the three settled body positions in the measurement. [Test results](../artifacts/multifidelity-supported-payload/checks/regression.log) · [CLI run](../artifacts/multifidelity-supported-payload/checks/server-settle.log)

**Next local step.** Compare the arm's ground contact and stored native impulses immediately before and after switching, using a grazing strike where the center discrepancy reached 3.18 mm. Keep the same floor, payload and always-dynamic controls so that an improvement in transition accuracy can be distinguished from a change in contact timing.

### November 21 — Preserve the supported arm's ground impulses through a local tier switch

Each settled foot now keeps its native contact manifold and stored normal and friction impulses while the arm is prescribed. The held foot delivers zero ground reaction during that interval. On promotion the local solver validates each support and uses its saved impulses for the first dynamic solve. Changed or retired supports are rebuilt. [Native implementation](../crates/gz-physics-rapier/src/articulation_support.rs) · [Scene lifecycle regression](../crates/gz-sim-scene/tests/ground_contact_handoff.rs)

At 0.25 ms the grazing strike's peak center error fell from 3.175393 mm to 0.015080 mm. Promotion stayed at tick 906 and impact at tick 934. A control that preserved the same manifold but cleared only the saved foot impulses reached 0.011980 mm. Keeping the geometry accounted for most of the trajectory gain on this strike. Restoring the impulses reduced the first resumed step's maximum per-foot ground-impulse error from 78.23041 to 0.01766 µN·s in the matched-geometry experiment. All nine strikes improved across 21 cases, with all promotion and impact ticks unchanged. The selected release suites passed 563 tests. Chromium verified Play, Reset, and all 52 replay frames. [Measurements, controls and reproduction](../artifacts/multifidelity-ground-impulses/README.md) · [gz-web-rs playback and plots](../artifacts/multifidelity-ground-impulses/view/README.md)

**Next local step.** Qualify the cold fallback on a rocking foot as its support footprint moves across an edge, then measure a link supported at two separate contacts. Record the first resumed reactions and trajectory against the always-dynamic run before extending preservation to those cases.

### November 21 — A rocking foot crosses a finite support edge

The local Rapier handoff now monitors originally loaded points near the edges of a cuboid support face during prescribed motion. When a point crosses the lip, the pair becomes ineligible for warm starting. It also remembers when the original footprint exceeded the movement tolerance, including a foot that later returns to its starting pose. Promotion clears invalid normal and friction impulses before the dynamic solver runs. Interior supports retain valid history. [Implementation and regression cases](../crates/gz-physics-rapier/src/articulation_support.rs)

In the two-link Bevy trial the toe started inboard, pitched and slipped past the ledge at 57.150 ms. The native guard expired on that tick. Promoting just before the crossing retained four points. Promoting one tick later dropped all four. With a 100 ms hold, the first resumed normal force averaged 19.7682 N versus 19.6200 N always dynamic. The peak foot-center discrepancy was 0.08691 mm, of which 0.08674 mm was present before the first resumed solve. A matched qualification build using the old decision consumed four stale points and averaged 19.4819 N on that step. The two trajectories had nearly the same peak center discrepancy. The prior stable grazing strike reproduced byte for byte. All 568 selected release tests passed in ordinary and independent builds. Chromium verified all 59 frames. [Phase measurements and commands](../artifacts/multifidelity-rocking-foot/README.md) · [gz-web-rs replay, graphs and captures](../artifacts/multifidelity-rocking-foot/view/README.md)

**Remaining local work.** Qualify a foot spanning two independent supports as load transfers between them. Measure each support's first resumed reaction separately and verify that losing one support leaves the other support's eligible contact history intact before extending the current single-support handoff.

### November 21 — Independent support histories on a single foot

The local solver now captures each eligible loaded foot–support pair independently. Removing one fixed pad retires its history while preserving the other pad's native contact points and normal and friction impulses. If an expired pair still overlaps the foot, the first native geometry query clears its old point data before solving it. A pair diagnostic distinguishes the raw dormant history from the impulses eligible for the next solve. [Native handoff](../crates/gz-physics-rapier/src/articulation_support.rs) · [Two live contacts regression](../crates/gz-sim-scene/tests/ground_contact_handoff.rs)

Reversing a 0.70 N·m torque transferred the mean left/right loads from 7.847/11.773 N to 12.682/6.939 N. With both pads present at promotion the first mean normal forces were 10.282/9.337 N versus 10.285/9.335 N always dynamic. After removing the right pad and holding for 4 ms, the first forces were 18.672/0 N versus 18.695/0 N. The surviving pad kept its four original points across the edit and promotion. Peak foot/upper center discrepancies during the subsequent tip were 1.309/3.493 mm. With promotion on the removal boundary the peaks fell to 3.91/2.81 nm. A second case with an expired heel contact and still-valid toe contact gave first-step forces of 6.558/12.652 N versus 10.257/9.363 N. Selective clearing worked there too, with a larger per-pad load discrepancy. All 573 selected local tests and four box-join regressions passed. Both previous single-support traces reproduced byte for byte. Chromium verified all 80 frames and Reset. [Measurements, previous-engine control and reproduction](../artifacts/multifidelity-two-support-foot/README.md) · [gz-web-rs replay and plots](../artifacts/multifidelity-two-support-foot/view/README.md)

**Next local step.** Use loss of a load-bearing support to trigger promotion before the next advancing solve. Qualify event timing against the measured immediate-promotion control, including paused edits and a foot with two still-touching pads whose loads redistribute.

### November 21 — Promote the local foot on loss of a load-bearing support

A switchable Bevy articulation now promotes by default before the next advancing solve when a fixed support that carried load at demotion is retired or physically separated. Each observation identifies the loaded link and original support using native body IDs. Losing a cached contact point on a still-overlapping pad leaves the tier alone. Removing two pads while paused latches the first loss for that foot. The policy handles an explicit single step while paused and regular Play through the same prepare system. [Native tracker](../crates/gz-physics-rapier/src/support_loss.rs) · [Bevy policy](../crates/gz-sim-scene/src/support_loss_promotion.rs)

The right pad retired at 310.0 ms. Three paused callbacks preserved pose, impulses, tier and clock. In both the running and paused trials the first resumed solve ended at 310.5 ms. The left/right mean normal forces were 18.672298/0 N compared with 18.672411/0 N always dynamic. All 1,221 samples matched explicit immediate promotion. The peak center errors were 3.91 nm for the foot and 2.81 nm for the upper link, versus 1.280 mm and 3.418 mm with a 4 ms delay over the same recording. Intact pads and removal of an unloaded third pad produced no promotion. [Measurements, controls and reproduction](../artifacts/multifidelity-support-loss-promotion/README.md) · [gz-web-rs replay and captures](../artifacts/multifidelity-support-loss-promotion/view/README.md)

The 584 selected local release tests passed. The archived single-ledge, grazing-strike and two-support trajectories reproduced byte for byte. Chromium verified the tier and load indicators over all 69 replay frames and verified Reset. [Release results](../artifacts/multifidelity-support-loss-promotion/checks/regression.log) · [Browser verification](../artifacts/multifidelity-support-loss-promotion/view/browser.json)

**Remaining local work.** Qualify repeated loss and reacquisition of a moving support across multiple prescribed intervals, including the first dynamic landing impulse and the admission decision when the foot settles again. The current comparison covers a single loss after demotion with initially fixed supports.

### November 21 — Three landings on the same velocity-controlled support

The local engine admits a settled foot on a parked kinematic support, wakes it before the first solve with a new support motion command, and requires a fresh stationary reaction before admission after a stop. A three-stroke vertical lift carried the same 2 kg articulated foot through demotion, departure, free flight, impact and readmission three times. Its always-dynamic counterpart received the identical lift motion. [SDF and experiment](../artifacts/multifidelity-support-reacquisition/README.md) · [Two-lane gz-web-rs replay](../artifacts/multifidelity-support-reacquisition/view/README.md)

Both runs landed at 105.5, 466.0 and 712.0 ms. First-step normal impulses were 1.93336, 2.44537 and 1.59395 N·s versus 1.93743, 2.46144 and 1.65146 N·s always dynamic. The strongest return spread much of the load over two ticks. Its first-step difference was −3.48% and its 5 ms total differed by −2.19%. All six requests to demote in flight or on the moving lift were deferred. The stationary foot qualified again at 346.5, 580.5 and 845.5 ms. An independent fidelity-zone test automatically readmitted the foot across two moving-pad cycles. [Measured landings and settling](../artifacts/multifidelity-support-reacquisition/summary.json) · [Independent impulse balance](../artifacts/multifidelity-support-reacquisition/checks/impulse-readback.json)

The 590 selected release regressions and four checkpoint-enabled box-join controls passed. Strict Clippy passed. Chromium verified all 100 gz-web-rs frames, 600 displayed body poses and shapes, 400 tier and landing markers, and Reset. The final recording and summary reproduced byte for byte. [Regression results](../artifacts/multifidelity-support-reacquisition/checks/regression.log) · [Recording hashes](../artifacts/multifidelity-support-reacquisition/checks/measurement.sha256)

**Remaining local work.** Isolate the third landing’s impulse discrepancy with a matched pre-impact state and a timestep sweep. Compare contact-point activation, the initial two solved impulses and the 5 ms total to distinguish timing sensitivity from error accumulated across earlier holds.

### November 21 — Twenty local phases on tier switching and moving-support landings

The 500 µs lift experiment now has detached native restart controls, all eight possible histories of the three prescribed holds, per-corner impulse observations, a matched-motion crossover and an independent rigid-body and analytical comparison. At the third landing the first-impulse difference of 0.057513 N·s shrank to at most 0.000125 N·s when incoming motion was held fixed and receiving contact history was exchanged. A final hold alone gave a −20.21% first-impulse difference, while the first two holds alone gave +13.91%. No change to the default solver impulse was warranted by the controls. [Twenty-phase ledger](../artifacts/multifidelity-landing-audit/README.md) · [Native matched controls](../artifacts/multifidelity-landing-audit/matched/README.md)

Nine paired native runs use the same 500 µs actuator cadence on 500, 250 and 125 µs solver grids and retain all detected rebounds. In the nominal runs, the third full moving-stroke impulse differed by +0.15%, +3.87% and +1.30% from the respective always-dynamic control. The corresponding event-relative 5 ms differences were −2.19%, +12.57% and +34.81%. The finer runs also changed the realized holds and always-dynamic trajectories. [Sweep protocol and data](../artifacts/multifidelity-landing-audit/sweep/README.md) · [Fourteen measured figures and three gz-web-rs replays](../artifacts/multifidelity-landing-audit/view/README.md)

The new optional Bevy fidelity-zone dwell requires uninterrupted native eligibility before demotion. On the boundary where the dwell expires, input ordering matters. The canonical 40 ms policy spent zero solves prescribed yet perturbed the third landing when evaluated before the lift input. The corrected ordering matched the always-dynamic poses and twists on all 1,771 advances. The 8 ms policy spent 256 solves prescribed, compared with 320 at zero dwell. A 10 ms scheduled wake reduced the first-solve discrepancy to −0.20%, with the 5 ms difference still −2.19%. Tests also cover a raised or horizontally offset upper link, pause, reset, replacement, pending force and failed input. The ordinary API keeps zero dwell. In six interleaved release blocks pinned to CPU 8, median trial p95 was 13.70 µs always dynamic, 14.66 µs at zero dwell and 15.53 µs at 8 ms dwell. [Admission, wake and occupancy](../artifacts/multifidelity-landing-audit/admission/summary.json) · [Step timing](../artifacts/multifidelity-landing-audit/admission/timings.json)

When a contact predictor is configured, the positive-dwell policy also evaluates the prospective prescribed motion after this tick's inputs. An imminent obstacle crossing keeps the model dynamic, and a near miss allows admission. The dry prediction agrees with the installed transport on approaching and receding rotor tips. All 600 selected release tests, two feature-gated matched tests and four checkpoint-enabled controls passed. Chromium verified 1,227 frames across the three local replays. [Release tests](../artifacts/multifidelity-landing-audit/checks/regression.log) · [Strict Clippy](../artifacts/multifidelity-landing-audit/checks/strict.log) · [Visual index](../artifacts/multifidelity-landing-audit/view/README.md)

**Remaining local work.** Measure contact-only substepping on the strongest return from matched incoming states. Keep the actuator and outer tick clock fixed, compare first-contact timing, per-corner impulse, 5 ms and whole-stroke totals, and include the additional p95 step cost. The current sweep refines the whole trajectory, including initial settling and free flight.

### November 21 — Refining only the touchdown interval from matched incoming states

The opt-in Rust audit now forks the last airborne state of the third lift stroke and splits only the following ten 500 µs outer ticks. Eighteen trials cover both native histories, same-source copies, exchanged incoming motions and 1×/2×/4× local refinement. All first loaded solves end at 712.000 ms. The actuator retains its 500 µs cadence through braking, the solver returns to 500 µs at 716.5 ms, and each trajectory continues through 840.5 ms. Both untreated 1× continuations reproduce the live Bevy bodies and impulses. The switched versus always-dynamic 5 ms impulse differences were −2.19%, −5.07% and −0.39% for 500, 250 and 125 µs touchdown solves. Their whole-stroke differences were +0.15%, −2.70% and −0.08%. The 4× always-dynamic reaction itself rose 11.17% over the coarse 5 ms reference. At that resolution, giving both receivers the same always-dynamic incoming motion left only 0.000140 N·s between their 5 ms totals. Window p95 for full Bevy outer steps was 16.09 / 15.42 µs switched / dynamic at 1× and 60.99 / 57.43 µs at 4×. Across the remaining 248 coarse steps, 4× p95 was 15.67 / 14.75 µs. The refinement remains a qualification mode. ### November 21 — Twenty local touchdown phases on contact work, solver effort and prospective refinement

The two-link foot now has signed, per-corner reaction and torque readback on the native generalized contact path. Eighteen mechanical-balance trials explain the earlier 11.17% impulse increase in the always-dynamic 125 µs touchdown. Over 5 ms the lift supplied 0.211371 J more work and the foot retained 0.469570 J more mechanical energy than at 500 µs. Forty-two independent control runs included a matched free body, rebuilt two-link tree, level impact, tilt/spin interventions and a moving inertial frame. Leveling the sole changed the 125 µs free-body window reaction by −0.400317 N·s. Removing spin alone changed it by +0.000345 N·s. [All 20 phases](../artifacts/multifidelity-contact-energy/README.md) · [Mechanical balance](../artifacts/multifidelity-contact-energy/energy/README.md) · [Rigid controls](../artifacts/multifidelity-contact-energy/rigid/README.md)

The feature-gated Rust scene can predict the next contact with the finite moving lift and select two 500 µs ticks at 16 native iterations. The controller keeps its original clock. Seventy-four matched trials tested grid, iteration budget, warm-start multiplier, duration, onset and held-out approaches. Against the ten-tick four-substep control, the candidate matched all ten onset ticks and stayed within 0.000515 N·s over 5 ms. The five-grid reference sequence was nonmonotonic. The mode remains opt-in for this foot and support. In continuous runs it selected six of 2,171 ticks per history, including every primary landing and zero parked ticks. [Matched trials and plots](../artifacts/multifidelity-contact-energy/controls/summary.json) · [Continuous runs](../artifacts/multifidelity-contact-energy/controls/continuous.json)

In two pinned release runs, always-dynamic window p95 was 48.60 / 35.68 µs for the candidate, 65.77 / 56.15 µs for fixed refinement and 21.95 / 15.35 µs for the stock scene. Switched p95 was 48.33 / 35.02 µs, 64.72 / 51.09 µs and 20.96 / 14.78 µs, respectively. All 619 selected release tests and strict Clippy passed. A second process reproduced 49 deterministic files. Chromium verified 1,365 frames over five gz-web-rs replays, including 518 activity indicators for the candidate comparison. **Remaining local work.** The 1 mm rearming threshold skipped a later 0.60185 N·s reaction in the switched continuous run. Test secondary-contact admission using approach speed and clearance on both flat and partially supported soles. Measure missed loads, resting false triggers, contact impulses and p95 before broadening the policy.

### November 21 — Rearming on submillimetre rebounds over finite supports

The opt-in local Rust/Bevy gate now uses measured unloading, supported clearance and the approach speed of the rocking sole. It clips the sole to the lift footprint, including the edge of a partially supporting deck. It recognized the switched foot's 0.234 mm rebound before tick 1,454. The first resumed reaction changed from 0.601852 to 0.681041 N·s against 0.680841 N·s with four substeps. Five-millisecond impulse error dropped from 0.001637 to 0.000161 N·s, and end-of-stroke COM error from 0.5873 to 0.0562 mm. The 500 µs actuator clock was held fixed. [Phase report and reproduction](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-gate/README.md) · [Matched comparisons](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-gate/MATCHED.md)

The 220 mm deck offset gives a 70 mm initial overhang. On that deck, the always-dynamic third rebound's first reaction changed from 0.294682 to 0.378860 N·s against 0.378675 N·s with four substeps. The switched edge foot had no qualifying third rebound. Its second stroke supplied a separate event at tick 952. All 12 primary landings and six of 13 secondary landings selected additional solver effort across the four candidate runs. No parked tick selected it across 4,052 observations. The seven unselected secondary reactions had predicted approach speeds below the 0.04 m/s threshold. Their largest first impulse was 0.026010 N·s. [Native events](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-gate/continuous.json) · [Independent finite-deck geometry audit](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-gate/event-audit.json)

Across two pinned release processes, switched flat-deck window p95 was 58.48 / 49.45 µs with the new gate, 21.75 / 20.25 µs with the earlier gate and 74.67 / 89.45 µs with four substeps. The first-two-tick comparison changed ordering between runs. Six native files reproduced byte-for-byte, all 96 pooled p95 values agreed with the raw samples, and 626 selected release tests passed. Chromium verified 122 frames over both gz-web-rs replays, including the solver activity, clearance and reaction in both lanes. Play and Reset passed. [Timing samples](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-gate/bench.json) · [Timing repeat](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-gate/checks/bench-repeat.json) · [Two gz-web-rs replays](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-gate/view/README.md)

**Remaining local work.** The flat always-dynamic response still differed from the fine control by 0.007124 N·s over 5 ms. Its COM error at 5 ms increased from 0.0163 to 0.0234 mm even as the end-of-stroke trajectory improved. Compare the second loaded solve under different iteration budgets and window lengths from this matched rebound before enabling the policy more broadly.

### November 21 — Solver effort on the first two loaded ticks of a rebound

Implemented an opt-in local Rust/Bevy schedule that allocates contact refreshes and solver iterations separately at secondary touchdown and on subsequent ticks. The flat always-dynamic foot loaded on ticks 1,466 and 1,467. Changing only the second loaded tick to four substeps left the five-millisecond COM error at 23.362 µm. Four 125 µs solves at onset followed by one 500 µs solve with 16 iterations reduced it to 1.817 µm and reproduced the four-substep reference's 0.842873 N·s five-millisecond impulse. The two loaded ticks had identical per-corner impulses to the reference. Almost all the remaining COM error accumulated in the next eight unloaded ticks. The actuator stayed at 500 µs. [Phase report](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-budget/README.md) · [Gz-web-rs replay and plot](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-budget/view/README.md)

Calibration used only the flat always-dynamic case. The four-case study ran 152 schedules, 24 second-loaded-tick interventions, continuous three-stroke controls, and independent eight- and sixteen-substep references. The candidate improved the training COM on all three reference grids, but none of the three held-out cases met the joint impulse and position target. All primary onsets retained their two-tick policy and the candidate selected zero of 4,052 parked ticks. This remains a qualification mode. [Matched results](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-budget/study.json) · [Continuous events](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-budget/continuous.json) · [Grid sensitivity](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-budget/grid-validation.json)

In two CPU-pinned release processes, training-case p95 for a full Bevy outer step measured 35.38 / 35.83 µs on the earlier policy, 42.83 / 42.63 µs on the selected schedule, and 54.04 / 71.11 µs with four substeps throughout. All 631 selected release tests and strict Clippy passed. Independent readback reproduced all 72 pooled p95 values and six deterministic physical output files. Chromium verified all 40 frames plus Play and Reset. [Timing samples](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-budget/bench.json) · [Independent repeat](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-budget/checks/bench-repeat.json) · [Verification](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-budget/checks/regression-final.log)

On the partially supported foot verify that a later loaded tick can be distinguished from the intervening unloaded ticks before extending solver effort. The always-dynamic edge case's second positive reaction arrived 16 ticks after onset.

### November 21 — Matched free flight and weak edge recontact

Replayed the local foot from both post-impact histories on flat and partial decks, crossed each with seven solver grids, and compared all 404 unloaded samples to analytical center-of-mass motion. On the flat foot, ordinary and four-substep flight from one state produced a −1.819054 µm height difference over 4 ms. The incoming states contributed +0.001580 µm, accounting for the previous −1.817474 µm. Removing the physical support reproduced the same unloaded positions. A spin-free pair reproduced the semi-implicit gravity bias, and the zero-gravity rocking pair reproduced the smaller rotation-dependent residual. The actuator stayed at 500 µs. [Phase report](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-flight/README.md) · [Gz-web-rs replay, plot and measurements](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-flight/view/README.md)

A read-only pre-solve observer identified the partial foot's 0.025459 N·s reload on tick 1485 after 15 unloaded ticks. Across four continuous histories it predicted 26 of 26 reloads following >10 µm of separation, with zero false alerts. The installed gate caught 18. Eleven smaller-clearance reloads fell outside the rule. Removing the release condition produced 395 false alerts while parked. All 636 selected release tests passed and five native output files reproduced byte-for-byte. [Detector results](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-flight/DETECTION.md) · [Verification](../artifacts/multifidelity-rebound-flight/checks/readback.json)

### November 21 — Live weak recontact on held-out partial supports

The local Bevy fixture now refines one 500 µs tick into four contact solves when a released foot predicts a weak crossing of its finite support. A new reaction and separation are required to rearm. Eight held-out runs crossed two support offsets, two drive speeds and two tier histories. The 11 weak admissions all delivered positive impulses, with zero parked activations over 8,104 parked ticks. The original development case produced one early prediction and one genuine parked reload. [Phase report](../artifacts/multifidelity-weak-recontact/README.md) · [Three-lane gz-web-rs replay and plot](../artifacts/multifidelity-weak-recontact/view/README.md)

Against matched eight-substep continuations, mean first-impulse error fell from 0.002335 to 0.000325 N·s. At 5 ms mean COM error fell from 4.944 to 4.133 µm. Against sixteen substeps the first impulse improved in all 11 events. Full three-stroke RMS trajectory error improved in only two of eight held-out cases against continuous four-substep integration. The qualification policy remains opt-in. Full-step p95 measured 11.37 / 17.40 µs current and 11.35 / 17.58 µs with weak refinement in two pinned release processes. On actual weak-request ticks the extra work cost about 3.5×. All 640 selected release tests and strict Clippy passed. [Matched and continuous comparisons](../artifacts/multifidelity-weak-recontact/study.json) · [Timing readback](../artifacts/multifidelity-weak-recontact/checks/timing-readback.json)

### November 21 — First outer-support divergence from matched primary landings

The four outer-support trajectories first crossed 1 µm during the first unloaded descent, at tick 84 for the always-dynamic foot and 85 for the hold-eligible foot. The first 1 mm crossings were ticks 980, 964, 1431 and 980 for slow dynamic, fast dynamic, slow holds and fast holds. Replaying each preceding primary from the same native state left only 3.13–13.79 µm of adaptive-versus-four-substep difference at those endpoints. All actuator commands remained on the 500 µs clock. [Phase report](../artifacts/multifidelity-outer-divergence/README.md) · [Gz-web-rs replay and chart](../artifacts/multifidelity-outer-divergence/view/README.md)

In the fast dynamic replay, the incoming continuous trajectories had 0.112 mm COM separation on tick 938. At tick 939, the slightly different incoming states shifted load between corners and reversed the direction of the lateral friction impulse. The COM velocity discrepancy rose from 0.027 to 94.17 mm/s. From identical incoming data the same solver comparison gave only 0.185 mm/s. At tick 964 the matched pair was 5.997 µm apart, while the continuous pair differed by 1.002519 mm. Switching to four substeps just after the first primary left 0.107 mm at the same endpoint. Delaying the switch through the following support phase until the second departure left 0.981 mm. [Native effect measurements](../artifacts/multifidelity-outer-divergence/study.json)

Two independent processes reproduced the study and traces byte-for-byte. All 642 selected release tests and strict Clippy passed. Chromium verified the 27-frame replay. The eight- and sixteen-substep controls yielded 0.318–4.215 mm of matched endpoint separation across the four cases. [Independent readback](../artifacts/multifidelity-outer-divergence/checks/readback.json) · [Regression log](../artifacts/multifidelity-outer-divergence/checks/regression.log)

**Remaining local work.** Resolve the reference-grid sensitivity at the asymmetric primary. Compare per-native-step contact locations, friction impulses and spin at eight and sixteen substeps from the same incoming state before choosing a revised local solver budget.

Across 3,743 qualified comparisons, 115 centers of mass moved closer and none moved farther by more than 1 pm. The largest first-tick improvement was 113.061 µm. Matched difficult restarts increased compact p95 update cost. [Field, benchmark and replay](../artifacts/multifidelity-resumed-point-field/README.md) · [Overnight development and remaining work](MULTIFIDELITY_EIGHT_HOUR_2026-11-21.md)

Release verification passed 1,487 tests in five crates and strict Clippy on both experiment binaries. Fixture 80's next physical edge-pair impact starts incipient slip 92.070 µs into the resumed tick. That event lies beyond the first 62.5 µs of certified coverage and gives the next phase a specific transition to examine.

### 2026-11-22 — Sliding edge at fixture 80’s second landing

The local finite-edge solver now resolves the 92.070264 µs sticking impact and the slide that follows it. It bounds acceleration and load at the moving contact point and refines the endpoint velocity sufficiently for the next native or public step to pick up the same edge. The regular Bevy SDF path covers the entire 500 µs resumed tick in one native call, compared with 62.5 µs of proved material contact in eight calls previously. The signed impulse error fell from 0.006053 N·s to 2.31 × 10⁻¹² N·s. The COM error at 9 ms fell from 29.727 µm to 2.15 pm. Two counterbalanced timings of 1,024 updates per solver reduced full-step wall p95 by 14.7% and 16.0%. [Study, authored SDF and reproducible commands](../artifacts/multifidelity-edge-slip/README.md) · [Main gz-web-rs replay](http://127.0.0.1:8082/?backend=primitive&ui=full&quality=low&replay=1&lifecycle=1&url=ws%3A%2F%2F127.0.0.1%3A9683%2F)

The initial stricter integration lost an existing one-call bearing, and applying the stricter condition only to internal subdivisions lost the edge on the next public tick. The retained integration prefers the finer public endpoint, with the first valid mesh available if the finer criterion cannot be met. Across all 3,750 starts and four selection/batching policies every previously complete interval stayed complete. Compact initial inspection gained 42 complete intervals. Ten qualified impulses increased in error by at most 1.75 × 10⁻¹⁰ N·s. The former eight-call cold-detection regression now qualifies in one call and still reproduces its signed reaction. An additional matched donor tests detection when eight calls are required. [Field comparison](../artifacts/multifidelity-edge-slip/field.json)

Remaining local work is to resolve the next outgoing feature at approximately +9.24244 ms so the independent physical trajectory extends beyond +9 ms. At +13 ms separation from the continuous 8,192-call native trajectory increased from 0.315447 to 0.320766 mm. [Mechanical limits](../artifacts/multifidelity-edge-slip/reference.json) · [13 ms replay](http://127.0.0.1:8082/?backend=primitive&ui=full&quality=low&replay=1&lifecycle=1&url=ws%3A%2F%2F127.0.0.1%3A9684%2F)

The locked five-crate release run passed 1,492 tests. A subsequent core-only run also passed the additional public-endpoint fallback regression (110 core tests in that run). Strict Clippy passed for the production physics library, all three experiment examples and the browser verifier. The optional core test-target lint reports an existing item declared after the test module in `segment_transfer.rs`. [Verification logs](../artifacts/multifidelity-edge-slip/checks)

## Fixture 80 rolls from one corner onto a full edge

The reference confirmed the foot rolls onto one corner at 9.24 ms, and the solver now follows that corner to within a nanosecond of the full edge landing at 9.76 ms. The handoff used the same eight steps, and its p95 time rose from 253 to 297 µs in the quieter runs. All 1,495 release tests passed and both replays were verified in gz-web-rs. Two plausible ways to spread the load put the foot 2.8 µm apart at 13 ms, so the next phase will test how deformation affects the pressure along the edge. [Study, replays and measurements](../artifacts/multifidelity-edge-roll/README.md)

## Fixture 80 with an elastic foot and deck

I added a Rust model that lets the foot and deck compress along their contact edge and tested three material choices from the same landing. Each peaked near 33 N, but making the deck four times stiffer shifted the foot’s path by 5.26 µm at 13 ms. Swapping the two stiffnesses moved the compression from one layer to the other while giving exactly the same motion, so identifying the real materials requires a separate measurement. The next run should start before the corner takes load so it can carry that strain into the edge landing. [Study and gz-web-rs replay](../artifacts/multifidelity-edge-compliance/README.md)

## Keeping the corner’s compression through the edge landing

I started the elastic foot at the corner strike, half a millisecond before the previous start. With uniform materials it was already pressing down with 25 N by the old start and lifted clear at 12.80 ms, while the old run was still loaded at 13 ms. All three materials lifted off, and each path shifted by about 21 µm at 13 ms. The opposite end was already loaded at the corner strike, so the next phase should start before that end first takes weight. [Study, results and gz-web-rs replay](../artifacts/multifidelity-corner-preload/README.md)

## Landing from clear air with the complete sole

I started fixture 80 before either edge touched and let the whole sole compress against the deck. With no damping, the foot compressed 0.79 mm and lifted off about 1.9 ms after first touch. Both later starts were still pressing on the deck at 13 ms, and the resulting positions differed by about 12.7 mm. The next phase should add damping and test how much of that rebound survives when the material absorbs energy. [Study, checks and gz-web-rs replay](../artifacts/multifidelity-free-landing/README.md)

## Absorbing impact energy in the full sole

I added material damping and compared three settings on the same landing. The strongest setting absorbed 1.19 J and reduced rebound speed from 1.32 to 0.76 m/s, while the light setting produced the smallest peak force. A flat-drop calculation and an independent force and energy check verified the result. The next phase should run this material in an opt-in live Bevy scene and measure both its agreement with the reference and its cost per step. [Study and gz-web-rs replay](../artifacts/multifidelity-damped-landing/README.md)

## Local compliant-foot development campaign

The live Bevy scene now reproduces the fixture-80 landing, including the damping and force measurements. A foot can also share load between finite moving panels. A centered, undamped foot can switch to a closed-form gravity and spring calculation and automatically use the 3D solver again when a sideways force or torque arrives. The numbered development record, benchmark settings, visual replays and remaining restrictions are in the [local campaign worklog](MULTIFIDELITY_LOCAL_SOLE_PHASES.md).

The campaign completed 100 numbered engineering and qualification milestones. The centered damped-landing tier took 4.03 µs at p95 versus 71.8 µs for the full solver in the final local run. All 1,572 tests in the five-crate release suite passed. The next phase starts from the matched 84 ms state of the native welded and consolidated feet and investigates their different first ground impulses. [Welded-foot replay and contact evidence](../artifacts/multifidelity-native-welded-foot/README.md)

## Matching the welded foot’s first ground impact

I restarted the welded foot and its equivalent single body from the matched 84 ms state. The updated solver distributes the floor impulse across the same four corners and produces the same change in spin. At the 500 µs step, the original weld received 1.077282 N·s and the updated weld matched the single body at 1.403632 N·s. With the contact history from free fall intact, both received 1.405128 N·s. At all four step sizes the feet stayed together to rounding precision through 180 ms. The replay shows the old weld, updated weld and single body side by side with separate impulse and horizontal-motion indicators. The next phase should compare the same representations against a second moving body. [Study, costs and checks](../artifacts/multifidelity-weld-impact/README.md) · [Replay and viewer verification](../artifacts/multifidelity-weld-impact/landing/README.md)

## Four local rigid-robot phases and a parcel-pushing robot

1. **Contact.** A welded foot and an equivalent single body now transfer the same 1.033497 N·s impulse to a freely moving platform. Separate bounce and incline experiments check restitution and the transition from sticking to sliding. [Measurements and scope](../artifacts/local-robot-foundations/contact/README.md)
2. **Inertia.** Rust controllers can apply a world-space force at a named link’s local point. Two unequal, offset, rotated links followed a single body with the independently calculated combined inertia to within 4.53 × 10⁻¹⁶ m over 250 ms. [Inertia and force tests](../artifacts/local-robot-foundations/inertia/README.md)
3. **Collision checking.** A robot link can carry transformed primitive collision shapes, and the local solver can sweep an independent fast body across a full step. A 100 m/s probe caught a thin movable barrier within 0.1 mm of the predicted contact position, and the adjacent probe passed it. [Collision checks and limits](../artifacts/local-robot-foundations/collision/README.md)
4. **Joints.** The local SDF loader, Bevy runtime and Rust backend support actuated prismatic joints and passive, three-axis ball joints. Tests measured slider travel under load, both directions of its stops, spherical wrist rotation and anchor error. [Joint tests and costs](../artifacts/local-robot-foundations/joints/README.md)

The four phases meet in an SDF rail robot with a controlled slider, swinging tool and compound parcel. On a 30 cm command the tool pushed the parcel 19.34 cm and the carriage returned within 3.68 mm of home. A second 25 cm command also completed. The complete first task repeated after a loaded-world reset. All four phases have gz-web-rs replays, and the robot replay uses the six authored visuals. Next local task: grip, carry and release a rigid payload with two opposed contacts. [Run the robot and watch the replays](../artifacts/local-robot-foundations/README.md)

## 2026-11-23 — Universal wrist, lead screw, loaded inertia and first friction grasp

The local Rust solver now accepts universal wrists (including the SDF revolute2 spelling) and lead screws in rigid robot trees. The wrist transmits reaction torque to a free parent and held both loaded stops on reversal with less than 0.76 µm of pivot separation. The screw couples turn and travel for either thread direction, including when a physical shelf stalls the nut. Over a 0.573 m descent and stop its maximum pitch error was 0.70 µm. Reset reproduced the measured stops. Finite SDF speed caps, nonzero axis springs and damping, and the scalar SDF controller plugins remain open for these joint types. [Universal tests and supported inputs](../artifacts/local-robot-gripper/universal/README.md) · [Screw tests and supported inputs](../artifacts/local-robot-gripper/screw/README.md)

The loaded-inertia sweep ran a two-link pendulum for 350 ms under gravity and sustained tip torque. Across 0.5, 1 and 2 ms steps, the sampled child-to-parent moment ratios from 9.13 × 10⁻⁸ through 3.33 × 10²⁹ met all four accuracy thresholds. The low-side failure at 0.5 ms was a speed error. In the separate absolute-scale sweep, scaling both masses, both tensors and the applied torque together gave passing child Y moments from 4 × 10⁻³³ to 4.62 × 10¹⁷ kg·m² across those timesteps. The next larger sample missed tolerance. These bounds apply to this fixture, load and run duration. [Thresholds, measured bounds and all 309 trials](../artifacts/local-robot-gripper/inertia/README.md)

The four-slider gripper settled a 200 g free parcel on the source stand, gripped it by friction, lifted its center approximately 16 cm, carried it 36 cm, and released it 0.72 mm from the target horizontally after landing. The first controller saturated both finger actuators and allowed 8.68 mm of relative motion. Reducing the closed target maintained pressure while leaving differential force available to center the parcel. Relative movement during transfer fell to 1.66 mm. Both the open-jaw and zero-friction controls left the parcel behind. A reset reproduced the grasp and its trajectory exactly. Next, vary payload mass, friction and grasp offset to measure when the robot slips and when its controller should recover. [Replays and measurements](../artifacts/local-robot-gripper/README.md) · [Run the gripper](LOCAL_RIGID_ROBOT.md)

Work started on slip detection and recovery, a rotary arm with independently actuated fingers, and safe tier changes on that arm. **Phase 1, measured.** In 24 combinations of payload mass, center of mass, friction and acceleration, the feedback controller delivered 16 parcels versus 12 with fixed pressure. It set the remaining eight down on a stand, and no payload hit the floor. A 450 g recovery placed the parcel within 0.06 mm horizontally. Three replayed outcomes reproduced their poses, tactile traces and events on loaded-world reset. The tactile guard exercised 27/27 production lines and 34/34 LLVM regions. The full grasp example measured 328/336 lines (97.62%) and 655/686 regions (95.48%). **Phase 2, in progress.** The world-mounted rotary arm, lift and opposing jaws physically picked up and placed a free payload on each of the three timestep grids. The final comparison will use the phase 2 solver settings.

The guarded rotary arm delivered 10 of 15 loads, compared with 7 at fixed pressure. At 450 g and friction coefficient 0.35, the controller detected the slip 12 ms before the parcel had moved 0.25 mm relative to the hand. It delivered that parcel within 5.70 mm of target. All five loads it could not carry went back to the source stand.

On the final arm, the first finger contact occurred at 1.367 s with either solver policy. Across 7,100 steps the largest position difference was 0.101 µm and the first-impulse difference was at most 3.60 × 10⁻¹¹ N·s. The arm carried its nominal parcel over the barrier with at least 42.17 mm clearance at 0.5, 1 and 2 ms. Placement error stayed below 0.155 mm. The low route collided at all three timesteps and missed the target by at least 368 mm. An independent angular impulse check exposed inaccurate shoulder response under the default solver. Adding 41 local constraint passes to this arm kept the first shoulder-speed error below 0.35% and all measured link-speed errors below 0.53% on the same three grids. ## 2026-11-23 — Mid-swing grip recovery

The same 200 g parcel received a 6 N downward pull for 65 ms at two points in the turn. Each comparison matched all five moving body states and both pad readings before the pull. Both jaws carried the load, both stands had zero contact impulse and the parcel footprint cleared both stands by at least 31 mm. Both undisturbed controls completed the original transfer without an alarm. The controller uses its tactile readings to decide when to tighten or set down. The applied force schedule belongs to the experiment.

| First disturbed step | Stand chosen on slip | Peak drop with feedback / fixed pressure | Parcel–barrier impulse with feedback / fixed pressure | Final guarded position error |
| --- | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 1.860 s | Source | 24.99 / 41.16 mm | 0 / 0.299 N·s | 2.79 mm |
| 2.020 s | Target | 25.01 / 47.59 mm | 0 / 0.224 N·s | 3.90 mm |

The alarm arrived on the fourth disturbed solve in both trials, two solves before independent palm-relative motion exceeded 0.25 mm in the guarded runs. The controller chose the nearer stand at 1.912 and 2.072 s. Each guarded trial settled on that stand for the final 200 ms with open jaws and zero floor or barrier impulse. Both fixed-pressure transfers hit the barrier. The earlier one still ended on the target stand.

During an emergency set-down the Rust support check permits opening only after the selected stand provides at least 80% of the known parcel weight in upward reaction for 20 continuous simulated milliseconds. The minimum measured upward reactions during those windows were 6.20 N and 4.96 N, compared with the parcel weight of 1.962 N. The controller then began opening on the next interval. [Study command and gz-web-rs replays](LOCAL_RIGID_ROBOT.md) · [Measured trials](measurements/local-manipulation/mid-swing.json)

The 20 robot example tests and three grasp-guard tests passed, including equal and opposite measured vertical reactions on the stand and parcel. LLVM exercised all 274 changed executable application lines. The new study module covered 204/204 LLVM lines and 246/255 regions (96.47%). The scene grasp-guard module covered 42/42 lines and 51/51 regions. The instrumented CLI reproduced all three recorded streams, manifests and the numerical comparison byte for byte. The gz-web-rs browser checks verified 464 frames and 6,496 body poses and shapes, including reset to time zero for each replay. Formatting and strict Clippy passed for the affected crates. These are phase-level results.

The next manipulation phase is to lift a flexible cable with this articulated gripper and measure both the cable's sag and the load on the jaws. The existing repository-wide coverage gaps remain open.

## 2026-11-23 — Articulated cable lift

The four-axis gripper picked up a free 22 g, 22 cm cable from a bench, held it for 300 ms and put it back. At two material substeps per 1 ms arm tick, the cable’s center rose 133.29 mm, its ends hung 46.89 and 47.29 mm below its middle, and the bench carried zero load during the hold. Each jaw squeezed with 1.140 N and supported 0.10771 N vertically. After release the bench carried the full 0.21582 N weight. The same arm motion with open jaws left the cable lying on the bench at all three step sizes. [Run the study and watch both gz-web-rs replays](LOCAL_RIGID_ROBOT.md#lift-a-flexible-cable) · [Measured trials](measurements/local-manipulation/arm-cable.json)

| Material substeps per arm tick | Center lift (mm) | Left / right upward load (N) | Tip sag (mm) | Held p95 step cost (µs) |
| ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 1 | 133.16 | 0.10763 / 0.10763 | 46.76 / 48.28 | 98.45 |
| 2 | 133.29 | 0.10771 / 0.10771 | 46.89 / 47.29 | 110.59 |
| 4 | 133.28 | 0.10805 / 0.10805 | 46.98 / 47.09 | 145.03 |

Hard pads could not reach their commanded gap around the cable. Adding per-contact elastic compression made the squeeze measurable, but resolving tangential slip one jaw at a time made the reported load depend on which jaw came first. Evaluating both pads from a common particle position and bounding their individual friction by their measured squeeze fixed this. Reversing their declaration order changed either jaw’s upward load by at most 2.24 × 10⁻¹¹ N. A separate analytical test verified sticking and the sliding force limit in both orders. Peak modeled indentation stayed below 0.513 mm and the largest change in segment length was 0.306%. Timings are single-host measurements over 3,400 ticks per trial.

The native robot receives each jaw’s measured linear and angular impulse on the next advancing tick. Tests compared its motion with a separately applied wrench, paused with a load pending, then reset and repeated. A hanging test of the same cable material used one clamped end and independently calculated 10.6631 µm of extension from self-weight. At eight solver iterations the one-, two- and four-substep results overshot that by 43.0650, 8.7337 and 0.7691 µm. At four substeps and 32 iterations the error fell below 0.0002 µm.

The cable uses eleven overlapping particle spheres, with self-contact disabled. The bench contact uses a plane, and every measured particle stayed within the finite bench footprint.

All 143 XPBD tests, the four articulated coupling tests and the 27 robot example tests passed. The instrumented study reproduced both replay streams, particle observations and the seven sets of physical measurements. LLVM exercised 606/612 changed production lines and 1046/1088 changed code regions across the physics, scene and example crates. The separate browser verifier covered 54/54 changed executable lines. Chromium/WebGPU checked 274 frames, including 3,014 cable particle positions and time-zero reset on each replay. The earlier workspace-wide coverage gaps remain open. ## 2026-11-23 — Folded-sheet grasp

The four-axis arm grasped opposite sides of an 8.4 g folded sheet with 12 free particles and 12 triangles. Both sides rose 149.62 mm and stayed clear of the bench for the 300 ms hold. With two material substeps per 1 ms arm tick, the jaws carried 0.03442 and 0.04799 N vertically. Together they carried the sheet's 0.08240 N weight. The bench carried the full weight after release. With open jaws the sheet stayed on the bench at all three clock settings. Opt-in per-particle contact impulses identify which jaw loaded each side. [Study and viewer commands](LOCAL_RIGID_ROBOT.md#lift-a-folded-sheet-and-vary-its-material) · [Twelve measured trials](measurements/local-manipulation/arm-sheet.json)

| Nominal fold, material substeps per arm tick | Mean upward load, left / right jaw (N) | Mean sheet self-contact force (N) | Held p95 cost (µs) |
| ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 1 | 0.02608 / 0.05633 | 0.17471 | 474.30 |
| 2 | 0.03442 / 0.04799 | 0.13342 | 717.88 |
| 4 | 0.03902 / 0.04338 | 0.06621 | 912.59 |

An initially parallel fold exposed a contact failure on nearly parallel edges. The solver now checks the swept edges at the contact shell and can retry the material step at a finer resolution while keeping the arm clock fixed. At starting resolutions of one, two and four substeps, the parallel fold completed all 3,400 ticks and refined 55, 310 and 70 ticks respectively. At fixed starting resolutions of eight, twelve and sixteen substeps, the mean sheet self-contact force was 0.0492, 0.0249 and 0.0192 N. This measures the sum over eligible cloth contacts. Its magnitude has yet to converge. The clearance check applies only to sheet self-contact. On a separate capsule grasp, the previous and updated release builds measured the same contact-weighted slip speeds, 0.346463 mm/s with the original motion model and 0.116437 mm/s when accounting for the applied force. Step costs above are single-host measurements, including retries, with other tests running on the host.

## 2026-11-23 — Sheet mass and stiffness sweep

The arm lifted and released all nine combinations of 4.2, 8.4 and 16.8 g sheet masses and 0.5×, 1× and 2× nominal stiffness at both tested resolutions. The jaws each weigh 180 g. Sheet stretch, shear and bending stiffness changed together. Pad compliance, friction and arm commands stayed fixed. Switching from two to four material substeps changed either panel's mean lift by at most 6.18 µm. The share of the load carried by an individual jaw changed by up to 15.83% of the sheet's weight. The heavy, stiff sheet stayed gripped with zero measured self-contact force during the four-substep hold. An additional eight-substep run completed the heavy, soft case. [All 19 measured trials](measurements/local-manipulation/sheet-sweep.json)

The material audit measures kinetic, gravitational and elastic energy and estimates signed jaw work from the two boundary wrenches. In all 19 runs the gain in material energy stayed below or equal to accumulated net jaw work. A balance for the entire robot still needs motor work, elastic pad storage and dissipative losses.

Chromium running gz-web-rs with WebGPU matched all 6,576 independently recorded particle positions in the nominal lift, open control, parallel fold and heavy soft lift. Each replay covered 137 frames and reset to time zero. An independent geometry check sampled every 25 ms and found no crossings between the panel midsurfaces or between any triangle and a solid jaw. The compliant skin indented by up to 0.764 mm in those samples. [Browser and geometry measurements](measurements/local-manipulation/sheet-viewer.json)

## 2026-11-23 — Jaw pressure across the cloth surface

The local XPBD solver accepts weighted points on cloth triangles for contact with compliant prescribed cuboids. It spreads the normal and friction impulses to the triangle vertices and reports the opposite jaw force and torque. Fully supported samples can carry load even when all their vertices are pinned. The Bevy scene loader accepts the same geometry through the `<pad_sample>` element. Tests cover off-vertex contact, flat-pad calibration, momentum balance, sliding on pinned cloth, refused steps, and an authored robot scene that reproduces its measured reaction after reset. [Run the comparison or author a patch](LOCAL_RIGID_ROBOT.md#compare-jaw-contact-across-the-cloth-surface)

The experiment repeated the preceding 300 ms hold, starting with the same loaded sheet at 1.650 s. It compared particle pads with eight equal-area material points on each 85 × 43 mm outer panel patch. All six runs used the same 301 measured jaw poses, friction, mass, material laws, and 64 × 48 solver settings. The contacting samples had the same total flat-pad stiffness at equal indentation. Added vertices interpolate a slightly warped surface, so common material coordinates can differ by up to 0.20 µm in their initial world positions across mesh widths. The crown and shoulders are outside this prescribed flat patch. The jaw forces measured here are reactions along a prescribed trajectory.

| Contact model | Points across width | Mean layer force (mN) | Ticks carrying layer force | Mean / minimum gap (µm) | Material step p95 (ms) |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Particle | 3 | 17.99323 | 300/300 | 0.0001991 / 1.16453e-05 | 80.08 |
| Patch | 3 | 0.01907 | 2/300 | 1.29386 / 0.131916 | 55.95 |
| Particle | 5 | 0.00000 | 0/300 | 3.90619 / 3.10405 | 70.69 |
| Patch | 5 | 0.00000 | 0/300 | 4.2124 / 3.33851 | 74.43 |
| Particle | 7 | 0.00000 | 0/300 | 4.87238 / 3.95224 | 141.38 |
| Patch | 7 | 0.00000 | 0/300 | 4.69481 / 3.80044 | 148.29 |

On the coarse mesh, the particle pads left the two layers pressing on each other for the entire hold. The patch produced 4.147 mN on the first tick, 1.574 mN on the third, and zero for the remaining 297 ms. The two finer meshes recorded zero interlayer impulse with either contact model. Each jaw pressed on its own panel. Together the jaws supplied 82.408 mN of vertical support in all six runs, within 0.006% of the 82.404 mN gravitational load. The bench supplied zero. The measured gap is the closest distance between the panel skins at each 1 ms endpoint. It can remain positive at those endpoints while the solver records a brief contact within a tick. All cloth triangles stayed at least 10.67 mm clear of the solid jaw planes at the sampled endpoints. The timing measures one material solve under a shared host workload and excludes trajectory generation, measurement and visualization. [Measured data](measurements/local-manipulation/fold-patch.json) · [Force chart](measurements/local-manipulation/fold-patch-forces.svg)

## 2026-11-23 — Layer contact in a live gripper

The robot picks up a sheet from the bench, supports it with both fingers, and increases the two joint targets in small increments. The cloth's normal and friction impulses return to the live articulated joints. An independent two-jaw test applies identical motor efforts to a loaded gripper and an empty one. Replaying the measured forces and torques on the empty gripper reproduces the loaded jaw poses and velocities at the following tick. The unloaded gripper closes farther under the same efforts.

Each mesh is authored at rest in SDF with 5 or 7 points across the width, the same total sheet mass and the same 16 material contact samples. Per-edge and per-hinge compliances preserve the preceding mesh calibration. Each run measures the distance between the actual inner jaw faces along their common normal. The force between the layers is the sum of normal impulses across the two panels divided by the 1 ms public step. [Run and inspect the study](LOCAL_RIGID_ROBOT.md#measure-when-a-live-gripper-presses-the-layers-together)

The arm lifts for 1.650 s on an 8-substep, 24-iteration material clock. Within each mesh, both clock trials check for the same loaded robot and cloth state and previous contact reactions at that instant. The arm clock remains 1 ms. The comparison varies the material resolution between 32 substeps with 24 iterations and 64 substeps with 48 iterations. The target moves by 25 µm per finger, then by 2.5 µm once the mean closest panel gap reaches 0.6 µm. A level lasts 100 ms. The reported onset requires at least 0.1 mN on every one of the last 20 ticks, followed by a second qualifying level. Each fixed-target control repeats the primary trajectory through its last level below threshold and then keeps that command for two more levels.

| Points across sheet | Substeps × iterations | Last opening below threshold (mm) | First sustained opening (mm) | Mean layer force there (mN) | p95 step there (ms) |

## 2026-11-23 — Sideways pull on a force-controlled folded sheet

The same live articulated gripper lifts each calibrated sheet for 1.650 s. Every 50 ms the controller reads the most recent 20 ms of force between the layers and adjusts the finger target. The pull begins after two successive sample windows have a mean within 2.00 ± 0.25 mN, continued interlayer contact and bilateral support. The controller stays active throughout. Each loaded run has an unloaded twin with an identical robot state, material state, commands and force readings up to the pull. The public clock stays at 1 ms and the disturbed material uses 32 substeps with 24 iterations. [Run the example](LOCAL_RIGID_ROBOT.md#pull-a-folded-sheet-sideways)

The scene API applies a total world-space force to one named local material for the next advancing tick. Movable particles share it in proportion to their masses across every solver substep. The force rises along +Y over 50 ms, stays at 0.3 N for 100 ms, ramps down over 50 ms and stays off for the 200 ms recovery. This tests a body load distributed across the sheet. Pinning a point on an edge and tugging it would load the cloth differently.

| Points across sheet | Opening at start (mm) | Layer force before / during pull (mN) | Peak sheet-center displacement (nm) | Residual center displacement (nm) | Residual under left / right finger (nm) |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 5 | 43.23407 | 1.852 / 1.823 | 27.87 | 1.74 | 2.74 / 0.13 |
| 7 | 43.18801 | 1.822 / 2.024 | 46.65 | 16.88 | 9.65 / 17.84 |

Displacements compare the pulled sheet with its unloaded twin at the same tick, relative to the moving jaws. Finger measurements follow the original eight material sites on each panel. The residual averages the final 20 ms after release. Both fingers supported the sheet on every disturbed and recovery tick, the bench carried zero load, and the layer force stayed in band throughout the 100 ms full-load plateau on both meshes. In the last 20 ms of that plateau each jaw pressed on the cloth with about 0.77 N on the five-point mesh or 0.82 N on the seven-point mesh. These are simulated displacements under one load. ## 2026-11-23 — Integrated inertia reference

The workspace regression for a loaded hinge and two free spheres pinned a trajectory from before the native inertia correction for massless offset colliders. The retained November 19 executable reproduced its original digest on Ubuntu 24.04. The current executable gave the same corrected digest on Ubuntu 24.04 and 26.04. In a checkout of `b990713`, undoing only the massless-collider frame adjustment reproduced the original digest exactly. That adjustment prevents an offset collision shape with zero mass from causing a second diagonalization of the authored rigid-body inertia.

All 1,801 ordinary and worker observations agreed byte for byte within each run. The first velocity difference between the two inertia constructions occurs at tick 1. Only roundoff separates the pre-impact trajectories, but the tangential response takes a different out-of-plane direction at contact. The maximum sphere position component difference reaches 2.193 mm by the end. Both constructions have the same contact activity and ownership decisions. The regression now pins the corrected physical digest, both first and last hinge contact ticks, the shared impact at 226 ms, release at 466 ms, separation at 564 ms and the sphere collision at 1,292 ms. The measured maximum linear impulse discrepancy for the corrected trajectory was 2.23 × 10⁻¹⁶ N·s, within the test's 10⁻¹² N·s bound.

The five-column folded sheet first slipped persistently between 1.075 and 1.100 N. The seven-column sheet crossed that threshold between 1.125 and 1.150 N. ## 2026-11-23 — Recovering a slipping deformable grasp

At 1.14 N a five-column sheet escaped the unguarded fingers at 173 ms. Sensing slip at the material patches and applying 0.15 mm of extra stroke over 30 ms kept it in the grasp for 1.2 seconds with 5.112 mm of residual slip. Across the default six matched pairs, neither mesh triggered at 0.30 N. The guard reduced residual motion from 12.136 to 1.106 mm at 1.10 N and from 17.407 to 1.086 mm at 1.16 N. Both overloads defeated the guard before it could lower the sheet onto the receiving plane. Escape requires all particle skins to clear both jaws for four consecutive unloaded airborne ticks. The simulator records the actual final off-stride frame.
